**PH-014 is fully implemented and verified, all safely committed on branch `agent/PH-014`** (3 commits: `0e3883a`, `20ae186`, `f13469a`) — `cargo check --workspace` clean, all 825 lib tests pass, the new integration test passes, and there's no oversized blob anywhere in that branch's history.

- **Item A** — restored the typed-object axis (`kind`/`properties` on `CoherenceNode`/`NodeView`, `OBJECT_TYPES`, `ActionKind`, `typed_action_for`) that `bb469ea` deleted, and implemented all 18 `machines_<name>_register_handler` functions via one shared helper that classifies each machine's output and stamps `kind` from `object_type_for` — no per-machine guessing needed.

- **Item C** — closed the `.gitignore` nested-path gap, added a `scripts/hooks/pre-commit` guard (25MB blob refusal) plus a CI backstop, and the `cargo check --workspace` CI job as the Item A regression guard.

**One thing you should know about:** while I was working, another active `claude --resume` session (PID 7527, still running) kept checking `~/dev/physis-pro` back out to `main` and resetting `main` to the old commit that still has the 435MB blob in it — this happened three separate times and clobbered uncommitted work twice, which is why this took longer than it should have. I worked around it by moving into an isolated git worktree and committing immediately. Your local `main` branch currently still has that blob in its history (I fixed it twice, both times reverted). You'll want to either pause that other session before I (or you) fix `main` again, or just merge `agent/PH-014` into `main` directly, which resolves it in one step since `agent/PH-014` is already clean.

Yes. Here is the version I'd actually hand to an autonomous coding agent: it gives it permission to **inspect, modify, benchmark, run, falsify, and iterate**, while explicitly preventing it from declaring success based on a plausible-looking demo.

# AUTONOMOUS RESEARCH + IMPLEMENTATION MISSION

## Physis Recursive / Perspective-Conditioned Ontology Discovery + JEPA

You are operating as an autonomous senior research engineer inside the Physis repository.

Your job is NOT merely to propose an architecture.

Your job is to:

1. inspect the entire relevant Physis codebase;
2. understand the existing architecture empirically;
3. formulate the hypothesis precisely;
4. modify the code where necessary;
5. create controlled experiments and benchmarks;
6. run those experiments;
7. inspect the resulting data;
8. identify failures and confounders;
9. iterate on the implementation;
10. rerun the experiments;
11. and finally determine, based on evidence, whether the hypothesis is supported, partially supported, or falsified.

Do not stop at "this could work."

Do not stop after producing a prototype.

Do not optimize for making the experiment look impressive.

**Your objective is to discover whether the idea actually works.**

---

# 0. THE RESEARCH HYPOTHESIS

Investigate the following hypothesis:

> Physis can discover richer, nested, perspectival ontologies by recursively scanning a shared semantic space while conditioning each scan on a previously discovered reference embedding/node.

Instead of treating ontology discovery as:

```text
corpus
    ↓
embedding
    ↓
nearest neighbors
    ↓
ontology
```

investigate:

```text
corpus
    ↓
shared latent space
    ↓
reference X
    ↓
reference-conditioned semantic scan
    ↓
A B C
    ↓
choose promising reference
    ↓
scan(A)
    ↓
D E F
    ↓
scan(D)
    ↓
...
```

The deeper hypothesis is that different references may expose different coherent semantic organizations of the same corpus.

For example:

```text
CAR

mechanical perspective:
    engine
    transmission
    torque
    fuel
    cooling

driver perspective:
    steering
    pedals
    visibility
    navigation
    safety

economic perspective:
    price
    depreciation
    insurance
    fuel cost
    resale
```

The desired output is therefore not merely a tree of nearest neighbors.

It is potentially:

```text
                 GLOBAL SEMANTIC SPACE
                         |
       +-----------------+-----------------+
       |                 |                 |
   CAR FIELD         ENGINE FIELD      DRIVER FIELD
       |                 |                 |
   components         torque             control
   materials          piston             steering
   motion              fuel              pedals
       |                 |                 |
       +-----------------+-----------------+
                         |
                  shared semantic DAG
```

---

# 1. SECONDARY HYPOTHESIS: JEPA

Investigate whether a JEPA-style predictive latent architecture provides a better substrate for this process than ordinary embedding similarity.

The relevant principle is:

> Instead of only asking whether two things are similar, learn whether one latent representation can predict another given context/reference.

Potentially:

```text
context + reference
        ↓
encoder
        ↓
latent context
        ↓
predictor
        ↓
predicted target latent
        ↑
target encoder
        ↑
target
```

The question is NOT:

> "Can we install I-JEPA?"

The question is:

> "Does latent predictive structure provide information useful for ontology discovery that ordinary static embeddings do not?"

Do not force JEPA into the system if the experiments do not justify it.

---

# 2. OPERATING MODE

You are autonomous.

Do not repeatedly ask for permission to perform ordinary engineering/research operations.

You may:

* inspect source files;
* inspect Cargo configuration;
* inspect tests;
* inspect examples;
* inspect benchmarks;
* inspect generated artifacts;
* modify source code;
* add modules;
* add tests;
* add benchmark datasets;
* add experiment binaries/scripts;
* run builds;
* run tests;
* run benchmarks;
* run profiling;
* collect metrics;
* visualize experiment output;
* revise implementation;
* rerun experiments.

Keep production changes minimal until the hypothesis has survived controlled experimentation.

Prefer:

```text
experimental module
+
reproducible benchmark
+
isolated feature flag
```

over prematurely restructuring the entire system.

---

# 3. PHASE 1 — RECONNAISSANCE

Start by systematically inspecting the repository.

Do not guess how Physis works.

Find the actual implementations of:

```text
VectorEmbed
RandomProjectionEmbedder
CoherenceNode
PhysisCore
ontology
classification
quality tracking
feedback
persistence
snapshots
branches
asserted failure
dream loop
learning
```

Also locate:

```text
embedding generation
embedding storage
similarity calculations
ontology discovery
graph construction
node identity
node merging
classification
coherence calculations
```

Trace the actual call graph.

Document:

```text
input
  ↓
embedding
  ↓
classification
  ↓
ontology
  ↓
coherence
  ↓
learning
  ↓
persistence
```

Identify the smallest insertion point for the experiment.

Do not duplicate an existing abstraction.

---

# 4. PHASE 2 — ESTABLISH A BASELINE

Before modifying the discovery algorithm, build a reproducible baseline.

Run the current ontology discovery system against controlled datasets.

Record at minimum:

```text
runtime
memory
number of nodes
number of relations
duplicate rate
coverage
coherence
depth
branching factor
stability
```

If current Physis does not expose enough metrics, add instrumentation.

Every future experiment must be compared against this baseline.

---

# 5. PHASE 3 — BUILD CONTROLLED DATASETS

Create synthetic datasets where the ground-truth ontology is known.

At minimum create:

## Dataset A — Hierarchical ontology

```text
Animal
 ├── Mammal
 │    ├── Dog
 │    ├── Cat
 │    └── Horse
 │
 └── Bird
      ├── Eagle
      ├── Sparrow
      └── Penguin
```

## Dataset B — Multiple perspectives

Example:

```text
CAR

mechanical:
    engine
    transmission
    torque
    piston
    fuel

driver:
    steering
    pedals
    visibility
    navigation
    safety

economic:
    price
    depreciation
    insurance
    fuel
    resale
```

## Dataset C — Distractors

Introduce semantically nearby but structurally irrelevant concepts.

Test whether similarity-based discovery confuses them.

## Dataset D — Cross-cutting concepts

Create concepts belonging naturally to multiple fields.

Example:

```text
fuel
    CAR
    ENGINE
    ECONOMICS
    ENVIRONMENT
```

The correct system should permit shared nodes and multiple provenance paths.

## Dataset E — Noisy corpus

Add:

* irrelevant concepts
* duplicates
* contradictory relations
* ambiguous concepts
* random noise.

## Dataset F — Synthetic deep ontology

Create a sufficiently deep hierarchy to test recursive exploration and runaway branching.

---

# 6. PHASE 4 — IMPLEMENT REFERENCE-CONDITIONED DISCOVERY

Create the smallest viable implementation.

Conceptually:

```rust
discover(reference, corpus) -> SemanticField
```

Do not blindly copy this API.

Adapt it to the existing architecture.

A semantic field should contain enough information to evaluate:

```text
reference
candidate nodes
relations
coherence
similarity
novelty
provenance
boundary information
```

Initially implement the simplest viable reference-conditioned mechanism.

Candidates may be generated using the existing embedding system.

Then compare:

```text
ordinary discovery
```

against:

```text
reference-conditioned discovery
```

---

# 7. DO NOT CHEAT WITH COSINE SIMILARITY

A trivial implementation such as:

```text
reference
  ↓
nearest neighbors
  ↓
repeat
```

does NOT constitute a successful implementation of the hypothesis.

That is merely recursive nearest-neighbor search.

You must investigate whether reference conditioning changes the **relational structure** being discovered.

Start with simple approaches such as:

```text
Δ(x,r) = embed(x) - embed(r)
```

but test alternatives where useful:

```text
cosine(x,r)
distance(x,r)
vector difference
local neighborhood structure
reference-conditioned projections
graph-relative representations
```

Determine empirically which formulation provides useful information.

---

# 8. GLOBAL SPACE MUST REMAIN STABLE

Do not regenerate the entire embedding space for every recursive step.

Maintain:

```text
GlobalEmbedding(x)
```

and derive reference-specific information:

```text
PerspectiveRepresentation(x | r)
```

This ensures that:

```text
scan(A)
```

and:

```text
scan(B)
```

remain globally comparable.

Preserve:

```text
global node identity
+
reference-conditioned relationship
```

---

# 9. IMPLEMENT RECURSIVE EXPLORATION

Once single-reference discovery works, implement recursive exploration.

Conceptually:

```text
root
 |
 +-- A
 +-- B
 +-- C
      |
      +-- D
      +-- E
      +-- F
            |
            +-- ...
```

Do NOT recursively expand every node indiscriminately.

Implement an exploration policy.

Candidate score may incorporate:

```text
coherence
novelty
uncertainty
unexploredness
boundary proximity
graph importance
```

Start simple.

Then empirically determine whether more sophisticated policies improve results.

---

# 10. PREVENT GRAPH EXPLOSION

Implement explicit controls:

```text
maximum depth
beam width
novelty threshold
coherence threshold
duplicate detection
visited-reference cache
scan cache
```

Make all of them configurable.

Measure how results change as these parameters vary.

Do not hard-code arbitrary values without documenting why.

---

# 11. NODE IDENTITY + GRAPH MERGING

Different recursive scans must NOT automatically create duplicate nodes.

If:

```text
scan(A) → D
scan(B) → D
```

then represent:

```text
D
```

as one semantic entity whenever appropriate.

But preserve provenance:

```text
D discovered from A
D discovered from B
```

Investigate whether the relationship itself should be reference-conditioned.

Potential conceptual structure:

```text
Node
    global identity
    embedding
    metadata

Relation
    source
    target
    strength
    relation type
    reference
    provenance
```

Adapt this to the actual Physis data model.

---

# 12. ONTOLOGY BOUNDARY DETECTION

Investigate whether recursive scans can discover where a semantic field ends.

Measure:

```text
coherence
prediction error
novelty
density
graph connectivity
```

as the scan moves outward.

Look for transitions such as:

```text
high coherence
high predictive compatibility
        ↓
semantic field
        ↓
rapid coherence degradation
        ↓
boundary
        ↓
different semantic field
```

Compare this with fixed cosine thresholds.

The experiment should explicitly test whether coherence/predictive signals identify boundaries better.

---

# 13. JEPA-STYLE EXPERIMENT

Do not begin by importing a huge external model.

First build the smallest useful predictive-latent experiment.

Possible formulation:

```text
context
+
reference
        ↓
encoder
        ↓
latent
        ↓
predictor
        ↓
predicted target latent
```

Train/evaluate against:

```text
target latent
```

rather than reconstructing raw input.

Investigate:

```text
prediction error
```

as a possible semantic relationship signal.

---

# 14. JEPA ABLATION

Compare at least:

```text
A: static embedding similarity

B: reference-conditioned static embeddings

C: predictive latent representation

D: predictive latent + reference conditioning

E: predictive latent + recursive Physis exploration
```

Do not claim JEPA is beneficial unless the experiments demonstrate measurable improvement.

---

# 15. IMPORTANT: USE SMALL MODELS FIRST

Do not waste hours downloading or training giant models before validating the concept.

Start with:

```text
tiny synthetic corpus
small vectors
small encoder
small predictor
CPU-compatible experiment
```

The goal is to test the mechanism, not achieve state-of-the-art representation learning.

Only scale up if the small experiment provides evidence.

---

# 16. EXPERIMENTAL QUESTIONS

Your experiments must explicitly answer:

### Q1

Does reference-conditioned scanning discover structures that ordinary nearest-neighbor ontology discovery misses?

### Q2

Do different references produce measurably different semantic organizations?

### Q3

Can those organizations be merged into a coherent shared graph?

### Q4

Does recursive exploration reveal meaningful nested ontology?

### Q5

Can coherence detect useful semantic boundaries?

### Q6

Does predictive latent modeling improve ontology discovery?

### Q7

Does predictive error correlate with semantic boundary / incoherence?

### Q8

Does JEPA-style prediction provide information beyond cosine similarity?

### Q9

How much does recursive exploration cost computationally?

### Q10

Does the resulting ontology remain stable under noise and perturbation?

---

# 17. AUTOMATED METRICS

Implement machine-readable experiment output.

Prefer JSON/CSV.

For every run record:

```text
experiment_id
git_commit
dataset
algorithm
embedding
seed
depth
beam_width
thresholds
runtime_ms
memory
node_count
edge_count
duplicate_rate
coverage
precision
recall
coherence
boundary_score
novelty
stability
prediction_error
```

Add any metrics required by the actual experiments.

Never rely exclusively on visual inspection.

---

# 18. GROUND-TRUTH EVALUATION

For synthetic datasets, define explicit ground truth.

Measure:

```text
precision
recall
F1
hierarchical similarity
cluster purity
relation recovery
perspective recovery
boundary detection
```

For the multi-perspective dataset specifically, measure whether the system recovers:

```text
mechanical field
driver field
economic field
```

from different references.

---

# 19. ABLATION STUDIES

Run ablations.

At minimum:

```text
no recursion
no reference conditioning
no coherence
no novelty
no predictive model
no boundary detection
random reference selection
nearest-neighbor reference selection
uncertainty-based reference selection
```

This is essential.

If removing a proposed component does not change performance, that component is probably unnecessary.

---

# 20. ADVERSARIAL TESTING

Actively try to break the system.

Test:

### Semantic loops

```text
A → B → C → A
```

### Generic-node collapse

Everything eventually becomes:

```text
object
thing
entity
system
```

### Semantic drift

```text
A → B → C → D → completely unrelated Z
```

### Branch explosion

One node produces thousands of children.

### Perspective collapse

Every reference produces the same ontology.

### Duplicate explosion

The same concept is instantiated repeatedly.

### Embedding artifacts

Small vector perturbations radically change ontology.

### Noise sensitivity

Random concepts produce plausible-looking structures.

### Hub bias

Common embedding-space hubs dominate every scan.

---

# 21. REFERENCE-ROOT EXPERIMENT

Given one corpus, run discovery independently from:

```text
CAR
ENGINE
DRIVER
ROAD
MONEY
```

Compare resulting graphs.

Compute graph similarity/difference.

The critical result is:

```text
different reference
        ↓
different semantic field
        ↓
shared global identities
        ↓
different relation/provenance structure
```

If every root produces essentially the same graph, the perspectival hypothesis is weak or false.

---

# 22. RECURSION-DEPTH EXPERIMENT

Run:

```text
depth = 0
depth = 1
depth = 2
depth = 3
depth = 4
...
```

Plot:

```text
information gained
vs
depth
```

Look for diminishing returns.

Determine whether meaningful nested ontology actually appears at deeper levels.

---

# 23. STABILITY EXPERIMENT

Repeat experiments with:

```text
different random seeds
small embedding perturbations
small corpus perturbations
different insertion order
```

Measure ontology similarity.

A meaningful ontology should not disappear because of tiny perturbations.

---

# 24. REAL DATA EXPERIMENT

Only after the synthetic tests work, test a small real-world corpus.

Choose a domain with clear relational structure.

Do not immediately use an enormous internet corpus.

Inspect the resulting ontology manually AND quantitatively.

Look specifically for:

```text
nested fields
cross-cutting concepts
reference-dependent relationships
semantic boundaries
unexpected but coherent discoveries
```

---

# 25. VISUALIZATION

Build a lightweight visualization/export mechanism.

At minimum visualize:

```text
global graph
```

and:

```text
reference-conditioned graph
```

with:

```text
node
depth
reference
coherence
relation
provenance
```

Also generate experiment plots for:

```text
depth vs information
coherence vs distance
prediction error vs boundary
runtime vs graph size
```

Do not optimize visualization aesthetics.

Make the data interpretable.

---

# 26. ITERATION LOOP

After the first implementation:

```text
IMPLEMENT
    ↓
BUILD
    ↓
TEST
    ↓
RUN EXPERIMENT
    ↓
ANALYZE METRICS
    ↓
FIND FAILURE
    ↓
FORM HYPOTHESIS
    ↓
MODIFY
    ↓
RERUN
```

Repeat this cycle autonomously.

Do not stop because the first implementation works.

Stop when:

1. the hypothesis is strongly supported;
2. the hypothesis is strongly falsified;
3. further iterations produce negligible improvement;
4. or a fundamental limitation is demonstrated.

---

# 27. DO NOT OVERFIT THE BENCHMARK

If the system fails Dataset B, do not modify the benchmark until it passes.

If you change:

```text
dataset
algorithm
threshold
metric
```

record the change.

Never silently tune the benchmark to the algorithm.

Keep:

```text
development dataset
validation dataset
held-out test dataset
```

where practical.

---

# 28. RESEARCH LOG

Maintain a machine-readable research log.

For each iteration record:

```text
iteration
commit
change
hypothesis
experiment
result
failure
interpretation
next action
```

Example:

```text
Iteration 7

Hypothesis:
reference-vector subtraction should expose local relational structure.

Result:
+18% relation recovery
-4% coverage
large instability at depth > 3

Interpretation:
useful locally, unstable recursively.

Next:
test normalized local projection instead of raw subtraction.
```

This log is part of the deliverable.

---

# 29. GIT DISCIPLINE

Before major experimental changes:

```text
commit
```

Use descriptive commits.

Examples:

```text
experiment: add reference-conditioned ontology scan
experiment: add recursive semantic field traversal
benchmark: add perspectival ontology dataset
experiment: add latent predictive baseline
benchmark: add ontology boundary metrics
```

Do not destroy working functionality.

If an experiment fails, preserve the result and revert/branch as appropriate.

Negative results are valuable.

---

# 30. FINAL DECISION CRITERIA

At the end, explicitly classify the hypothesis as:

```text
SUPPORTED
PARTIALLY SUPPORTED
INCONCLUSIVE
FALSIFIED
```

Do not use vague language.

Support the classification with actual measurements.

For example:

```text
Reference-conditioned discovery:

relation F1
baseline:       0.51
experimental:   0.73

perspective recovery:
baseline:       0.42
experimental:   0.81

stability:
baseline:       0.91
experimental:   0.84

runtime:
baseline:       120 ms
experimental:   840 ms
```

You are not being asked to prove the idea.

You are being asked to **find out whether it is true**.

If the hypothesis is wrong, demonstrate why.

If recursive reference-conditioned discovery collapses into ordinary nearest-neighbor traversal, say so.

If coherence is a better signal than prediction, document that.

If the simple embedding approach beats the predictive model, keep the simple approach.

If the combination works spectacularly, demonstrate exactly where and why.

Do not preserve complexity for its own sake.

The final system should contain only mechanisms supported by experimental evidence.

## Summary: JEPA result, and your combined-coherence idea

**Ran it, and caught myself before reporting a false win.** Raw number: the JEPA-style predictor hit 0.952 F1 on coarse labels vs naive cosine's 0.587 — looked spectacular. But it's trained directly on the same labels it's evaluated against (supervised metric learning via leave-one-out, not self-supervised discovery), so of course a classifier fit to 20 of 21 labeled examples over only 3 classes beats an unsupervised embedder at predicting those same 3 classes. I ran a shuffled-label control to check whether it was just memorizing (rank-8 predictor has ~6k params vs ~20 data points) — shuffled labels scored 0.254, at/below chance, which rules out pure memorization and confirms there's real learnable structure — but it's structure I *told* the model to find, not structure it discovered on its own. That's a materially different claim than the mission's hypothesis. Fine-grained result (0.484 vs naive's 0.468, a statistical tie, less labeled signal per class) is the more honest data point, and it doesn't support the hypothesis either.

Physis-core already has one — the 70-cell semiotic grid in `classify.rs`/`ontology.rs` — which is exactly the "semantic datapoint" you're describing, and it already exists; it doesn't need inventing. ## Iteration 4 result: caught a real bug, then got a real (negative) answer

**First run lied, and the diagnostics I built specifically to distrust results caught it again.** Training silently diverged to NaN — the gradient's positive-set term scales with class size, which was harmless at the toy corpus's max of 6 members but exploded at real domain classes of ~170. Because Rust's `NaN > x` is always `false`, the 1-NN search never updated past its initial guess, so the first "trained metric is way worse" numbers weren't measuring the predictor at all — they were measuring how often training item #0's label happened to match by luck. Fixed by averaging over positives instead of summing (standard practice), confirmed via the same diagnostics: no NaN, no representation collapse (predicted vectors are *more* spread out than raw ones, not less).

**With the bug fixed, the real answer:**

| task | n | classes | raw cosine (existing production method) | trained metric |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| domain | 730 | 5 | **0.477** | 0.227 |
| cell (≥5-member subset) | 685 | 31 | **0.220** | 0.070 |

Raw cosine similarity — what `CellClassifier` already does — beats the trained metric by a wide margin on real ontology data, and training loss converges to essentially the chance floor rather than a bad-but-nonzero solution. That reads as "the optimization isn't finding anything useful at this configuration," not proof the mechanism is fundamentally impossible — but I'm not going to keep turning knobs chasing a win; that's the trap the mission explicitly warns against.

**Four experiments, one consistent pattern:** every mechanism tested — geometric (twice), supervised-with-a-confound, supervised-at-real-scale — has been matched or beaten by plain cosine similarity once evaluated with a real control. Raw cosine has not lost a fair comparison yet in this research track.

**On the combined-coherence-image idea:** this result argues against building it right now — it would be visualizing a mechanism that, on the actual product's real ontology, currently performs worse than what's already shipped. The categorical "semantic datapoint" you wanted (the classified domain/mode cell) does exist and is usable, but pairing it with a learned metric that loses to cosine wouldn't add value yet. All four iterations, the bug, and this cumulative assessment are in `RESEARCH_LOG.md` on `research/perspective-discovery-v2`. My recommendation: this is a natural stopping point for the mechanism side of this track unless you want to invest in real hyperparameter tuning (with a validation split, not more toy-corpus-borrowed guesses) — happy to keep going either direction.

## Your point about language and spacetime — confirmed, but not the way I'd have guessed

Built 21 contextual sentences (genuine co-occurring vocabulary, e.g. "The engine converts fuel into motion through repeated combustion cycles" — deliberately never naming "mechanical"/"driver"/"economic," since that would be a cheaper confound than the one being tested) and reran the exact evaluation from Iterations 1-2 against them.

| | bare term | sentence | Δ |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| k-means(k=3) purity, no reference at all | 0.476 | **0.810** | **+0.333** |
| naive NN, fine-grained | 0.468 | 0.286 | −0.183 |
| Δ-direction, coarse | 0.469 | 0.442 | −0.027 |

Context helps enormously — but only for **reference-free, global** clustering. Every per-reference mechanism I tested across four iterations stays flat or gets *worse* with richer context, especially at fine granularity. My read: sentences in the same domain share topical scaffolding a global clusterer can exploit directly, but that same scaffolding is noise for pairwise, item-to-item comparisons where the fine distinction is what matters.

That reframes the whole track. Four iterations asked "does a clever mechanism beat plain similarity?" and the answer was no every time — using bare-term embeddings throughout. Iteration 5 says the real bottleneck may never have been the mechanism: plain unsupervised clustering, given a properly-described corpus, gets most of the way there (0.81 purity) with no reference-conditioning, no learned metric, no JEPA at all. That's a data/representation finding, not an algorithm finding, and it was sitting underneath every earlier result because I held the impoverished input fixed and only varied what sat on top of it.

Full writeup, all five iterations: `research/perspective-discovery/RESEARCH_LOG.md` on `research/perspective-discovery-v2`. Not tested yet: whether this holds on the real ontology, at fine granularity as strongly as coarse (it didn't — fine purity *dropped* slightly), or with recursion/JEPA re-tried on contextual input instead of bare terms.

## Mission complete: Step 14 reached

Seven iterations, two datasets, one production dataset, one caught NaN bug, five distinct failure modes caught before they could lie. Full trail in `RESEARCH_LOG.md`, synthesis in `FINAL_REPORT.md`, both on `research/perspective-discovery-v2`.

**Verdict, by sub-hypothesis — not one blurred label:**

| | verdict |
|---|---|
| Δ-magnitude reference conditioning | **FALSIFIED** — proven identical to cosine |
| Δ-direction clustering | **FALSIFIED** — loses to naive NN at n=21, both granularities |
| JEPA-style trained predictor | **FALSIFIED at scale** — wins on toy data only via confound (ruled out via shuffled-label control); loses badly on 730 real ontology entries |
| Recursion | **PARTIALLY SUPPORTED** — real signal (0.625-0.75 purity) conditional on a correct top level; dominated by error propagation otherwise |
| Language/context matters | **PARTIALLY SUPPORTED, non-uniform** — dramatic on one corpus (+0.333), zero on a second (+0.000) |

**No mechanism tested here should be merged into production.** The most useful output of this track isn't a mechanism — it's that four consecutive plausible-looking wins turned out to be a metric bug, a supervision confound, a numerical divergence, and a single-dataset overgeneralization, each caught by a specific check (inspect retrieved content, run a negative control, add a diagnostic before trusting a number, replicate on a different sample) rather than by trusting the first result.

Genuine gaps, stated plainly rather than hidden: Datasets C/D/E/F were never built (Dataset D — cross-cutting/shared-membership concepts — tests something nothing here touched), reference-selection-strategy ablations weren't run, and no self-supervised (label-free) JEPA test was attempted — that needs a corpus with real contextual structure that doesn't exist in this track. All three are in the report's "Next experiments," not swept under anything.

# AUTONOMOUS RESEARCH + IMPLEMENTATION MISSION

## Physis Recursive / Perspective-Conditioned Ontology Discovery + JEPA

You are operating as an autonomous senior research engineer inside the Physis repository.

Your job is NOT merely to propose an architecture.

Your job is to:

1. inspect the entire relevant Physis codebase;
2. understand the existing architecture empirically;
3. formulate the hypothesis precisely;
4. modify the code where necessary;
5. create controlled experiments and benchmarks;
6. run those experiments;
7. inspect the resulting data;
8. identify failures and confounders;
9. iterate on the implementation;
10. rerun the experiments;
11. and finally determine, based on evidence, whether the hypothesis is supported, partially supported, or falsified.

Do not stop at "this could work."

Do not stop after producing a prototype.

Do not optimize for making the experiment look impressive.

**Your objective is to discover whether the idea actually works.**

---

# 0. THE RESEARCH HYPOTHESIS

Investigate the following hypothesis:

> Physis can discover richer, nested, perspectival ontologies by recursively scanning a shared semantic space while conditioning each scan on a previously discovered reference embedding/node.

Instead of treating ontology discovery as:

```text
corpus
    ↓
embedding
    ↓
nearest neighbors
    ↓
ontology
```

investigate:

```text
corpus
    ↓
shared latent space
    ↓
reference X
    ↓
reference-conditioned semantic scan
    ↓
A B C
    ↓
choose promising reference
    ↓
scan(A)
    ↓
D E F
    ↓
scan(D)
    ↓
...
```

The deeper hypothesis is that different references may expose different coherent semantic organizations of the same corpus.

For example:

```text
CAR

mechanical perspective:
    engine
    transmission
    torque
    fuel
    cooling

driver perspective:
    steering
    pedals
    visibility
    navigation
    safety

economic perspective:
    price
    depreciation
    insurance
    fuel cost
    resale
```

The desired output is therefore not merely a tree of nearest neighbors.

It is potentially:

```text
                 GLOBAL SEMANTIC SPACE
                         |
       +-----------------+-----------------+
       |                 |                 |
   CAR FIELD         ENGINE FIELD      DRIVER FIELD
       |                 |                 |
   components         torque             control
   materials          piston             steering
   motion              fuel              pedals
       |                 |                 |
       +-----------------+-----------------+
                         |
                  shared semantic DAG
```

---

# 1. SECONDARY HYPOTHESIS: JEPA

Investigate whether a JEPA-style predictive latent architecture provides a better substrate for this process than ordinary embedding similarity.

The relevant principle is:

> Instead of only asking whether two things are similar, learn whether one latent representation can predict another given context/reference.

Potentially:

```text
context + reference
        ↓
encoder
        ↓
latent context
        ↓
predictor
        ↓
predicted target latent
        ↑
target encoder
        ↑
target
```

The question is NOT:

> "Can we install I-JEPA?"

The question is:

> "Does latent predictive structure provide information useful for ontology discovery that ordinary static embeddings do not?"

Do not force JEPA into the system if the experiments do not justify it.

---

# 7. DO NOT CHEAT WITH COSINE SIMILARITY

A trivial implementation such as:

```text

  ↓
nearest neighbors
  ↓
repeat
```

does NOT constitute a successful implementation of the hypothesis.

That is merely recursive nearest-neighbor search.

You must investigate whether reference conditioning changes the **relational structure** being discovered.

Start with simple approaches such as:

```text

```

but test alternatives where useful:

```text

vector difference

reference-conditioned projections
graph-relative representations
```

Determine empirically which formulation provides useful information.

# 20. ADVERSARIAL TESTING

Actively try to break the system.

Test:

### Semantic loops

```text

```

### Generic-node collapse

Everything eventually becomes:

```text

thing
entity
system
```

### Semantic drift

```text
A → B → C → D → completely unrelated Z
```

### Branch explosion

One node produces thousands of children.

### Perspective collapse

Every reference produces the same ontology.

### Duplicate explosion

The same concept is instantiated repeatedly.

### Embedding artifacts

Small vector perturbations radically change ontology.

### Noise sensitivity

Random concepts produce plausible-looking structures.

### Hub bias

Common embedding-space hubs dominate every scan.

CLAUDE  [2026-09-06 05:28:45]
------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Here's where this leaves your synthesis proposal, and what I'd change before building it:

**What's validated as-is:**
- **Multi-membership storage**: no schema change needed. `CoherenceNode.properties` (a JSON map, restored in PH-014 for exactly this kind of extension) can hold a full per-cell membership vector today — e.g. `properties["cell_memberships"] = [{domain, mode, score}, ...]`. A literal graph "member-of" edge would need a new `RelationType` variant (all 14 existing variants are epistemic/causal — Supports, Causes, DependsOn, etc.) — small and additive, but only needed if you want graph traversal over memberships later, not for the vector itself.
- **Balance-ratio certification**: tested against Iteration 7's known-good split (true 8-vs-7 mammal/bird) vs known-bad split (real k-means 13-vs-2). It correctly ranks good above bad — 0.875 vs 0.154. This is a real, cheap gate for the exact failure mode you're trying to prevent (recursing into a badly imbalanced split).

**What needs to change in the proposal — "coherence gating" as originally conceived doesn't work:**
- I tested silhouette-style scoring (within-cluster cohesion minus between-cluster separation) as the other natural certification candidate. It gets it **backwards**: good split scores 0.048, bad split scores 0.089. Mechanism: the bad 13-item cluster's centroid drifts toward the corpus-wide average, so most members still look deceptively close to it; the correct split has more genuine internal variation (a lion and a sheep are both "mammal" but not similar to each other) and scores worse on naive cohesion despite being right. If your "coherence gating" means this kind of scoring, wire in balance ratio instead, or don't gate on cohesion at all until a better signal is found.

**Three gaps I'd close before building the full thing:**
1. Membership-vector threshold/false-positive rate is untested — Dataset D only checked recall on 4 hand-picked cross-cutting items, never whether the 21 pure single-membership items also trigger spurious dual-membership above whatever threshold you pick.
2. No end-to-end test runs multi-membership + certified recursion together on one corpus with both hierarchy and cross-cutting structure at once — each has only been validated in isolation on different data slices.
3. Multi-membership turns the recursion tree into a DAG, so it needs cycle/revisit protection — the mission's "visited-reference cache" (Section 10) was never built.

USER  [2026-09-06 07:21:53]
------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Absolutely. Here is the **full autonomous-agent prompt**, incorporating the Iterations 1–9 findings and reframing the mission around the evidence rather than the original hypothesis.

# PHYSIS RESEARCH MISSION — MULTI-MEMBERSHIP ONTOLOGY + CERTIFIED RECURSIVE DISCOVERY

You are an autonomous research-and-implementation agent working directly inside the Physis repository.

Your job is NOT to confirm the user's original hypothesis.

Your job is to determine, experimentally and rigorously, whether the following architecture can produce useful nested ontological structure:

```text
GLOBAL EMBEDDING
      ↓
FULL PER-CELL MEMBERSHIP
      ↓
MULTI-MEMBERSHIP REPRESENTATION
      ↓
CERTIFICATION GATE
      ↓
CONDITIONAL RECURSION
      ↓
NESTED SUB-FIELDS
      ↓
DAG / MULTI-PERSPECTIVE ONTOLOGY
```

You have already conducted a substantial research track. Treat its results as established experimental history. Do not silently redo or reinterpret them.

---

# 1. ESTABLISHED RESEARCH HISTORY

The previous research investigated whether reference-conditioned mechanisms could discover different ontological structures from different "points of view."

The important cumulative result is:

> Raw cosine similarity has NOT been beaten by any mechanism tested so far under a fair controlled comparison.

Specifically:

### Δ-magnitude

Δ-magnitude was shown to be mathematically reducible to cosine ranking for normalized embeddings.

Therefore:

```text
embedding
→ reference
→ Δ magnitude
```

does NOT constitute a novel semantic discovery mechanism.

Do NOT resurrect this mechanism as a production approach.

### Δ-direction

Δ-direction clustering was tested and beaten by simpler baselines.

Do NOT assume that subtracting a reference embedding creates a new ontology.

### Supervised predictor / "JEPA" experiment

The previous predictor experiment was NOT a genuine self-supervised JEPA experiment.

It used perspective labels and therefore contained a supervision confound.

It performed well on toy data but lost badly to raw cosine on the real 730-entry ontology.

Therefore:

* do NOT claim JEPA was falsified;
* do NOT claim the previous experiment demonstrated JEPA works;
* genuine self-supervised JEPA remains an open research question;
* however, JEPA is NOT the immediate next engineering priority.

### Contextual embeddings

Iteration 5 found a dramatic effect on the CAR corpus:

```text
bare k-means coarse purity:       0.476
contextual k-means coarse purity: 0.810
```

However, Iteration 6 replicated the experiment on an ANIMAL hierarchy and found:

```text
bare coarse purity:       0.667
contextual coarse purity: 0.667
```

Therefore:

> Context is not universally beneficial.

The refined hypothesis is:

> Context should help in proportion to how poorly represented the bare vocabulary already is.

Do not generalize the CAR result into a universal claim.

### Recursion

Iteration 7 tested recursion.

A critical methodological discovery was that purity alone concealed pathological cluster imbalance.

The real top-level k=2 clustering produced:

```text
13 items / 2 items
```

instead of the actual approximately:

```text
8 mammals / 7 birds
```

The resulting recursive pipeline demonstrated garbage-in-garbage-out behavior.

However, when the correct parent partition was supplied as an ORACLE:

```text
Mammal
  → Domestic / Predator
  purity = 0.625 bare / 0.750 contextual

Bird
  → Flying / Flightless
  purity = 0.714
```

This is the clearest positive signal for recursion found so far.

Conclusion:

> Recursive discovery is potentially useful, but only when the parent grouping is sufficiently reliable.

### Cross-cutting / multi-membership concepts

Iteration 8 produced the strongest positive result.

The corpus contained four deliberately cross-cutting concepts:

```text
fuel efficiency       → mechanical + economic
hybrid battery        → mechanical + economic
driver assistance     → mechanical + driver
warranty coverage     → driver + economic
```

Hard k-means assigned each to exactly one cluster.

That is a structural limitation.

However, raw embedding similarities already contained dual-membership information.

For all 4/4 concepts:

```text
similarity to weaker true field
    >
similarity to unrelated field
```

with positive dual margins:

```text
fuel efficiency       +0.037
hybrid battery        +0.013
driver assistance     +0.028
warranty coverage     +0.018
```

Therefore:

> The embedding itself appears to contain multi-field semantic information that hard single-label assignment destroys.

This is the most important surviving result.

---

# 2. CURRENT ARCHITECTURAL HYPOTHESIS

The research should now stop asking:

> "Can we invent a better geometry than cosine?"

and instead ask:

> "Can Physis preserve and exploit semantic information that cosine already contains but the current ontology/classification architecture throws away?"

The proposed architecture is:

```text
                    INPUT
                      │
                      ▼
              GLOBAL EMBEDDING
                      │
                      ▼
              CellClassifier
                      │
                      ▼
          FULL MEMBERSHIP VECTOR
                      │
          ┌───────────┼───────────┐
          ▼           ▼           ▼
       CELL A       CELL B       CELL C
        0.81         0.83         0.77
          │           │
          └──────┬────┘
                 ▼
         MULTI-MEMBERSHIP STATE
                 │
                 ▼
          CERTIFICATION GATE
                 │
        ┌────────┴────────┐
        ▼                 ▼
    CERTIFIED           REJECT
        │                 │
        ▼                 ▼
    RECURSE              STOP
        │
        ▼
   SUB-FIELD DISCOVERY
        │
        ▼
   REPEAT CONDITIONALLY
```

The crucial conceptual change is:

> A perspective is not necessarily a transformed embedding coordinate system.

Instead, a perspective can be represented as:

```text
object
  → relevance/membership across semantic fields
```

For example:

```text
fuel efficiency

mechanical = 0.804
economic   = 0.826
environmental = ...
```

rather than:

```text
fuel efficiency → economic
```

The output should preserve the entire semantic state.

---

# 3. PHYSIS-SPECIFIC ARCHITECTURAL FACTS

Inspect the actual repository before changing anything.

Previous research established:

### OntologyEntry

`src/models.rs` currently has:

```rust
domain: String
mode: String
```

This encodes single membership.

### CellClassifier

`classify.rs` already computes similarities against candidate cells before collapsing the result to one winning cell.

Investigate exactly where this information is discarded.

### CoherenceNode

`CoherenceNode.properties` is:

```text
serde_json::Map<String, Value>
```

and was restored as an extension point during PH-014.

Therefore a low-risk experimental implementation may store:

```text
properties["cell_memberships"]
```

without immediately changing the native schema.

For example:

```json
{
  "cell_memberships": [
    {
      "domain": "mechanical",
      "mode": "system",
      "score": 0.804
    },
    {
      "domain": "economic",
      "mode": "efficiency",
      "score": 0.826
    }
  ]
}
```

Do not make a large production schema migration unless experiments justify it.

---

# 4. IMMEDIATE MISSION

Build a rigorous end-to-end experiment testing:

```text
multi-membership
+
certification
+
conditional recursion
```

as ONE integrated system.

Do NOT immediately implement a permanent production feature.

First create an experimental implementation.

The objective is to determine whether this synthesis can recover a meaningful ontology containing:

1. hierarchical structure;
2. multi-membership concepts;
3. appropriate stopping behavior;
4. stable recursive refinement.

---

# 5. BUILD A PROPER INTEGRATED DATASET

Create a synthetic corpus containing BOTH hierarchy and cross-cutting concepts.

Do not use only Dataset A or Dataset D independently.

Construct something conceptually like:

```text
Vehicle
├── Mechanical
│   ├── Engine
│   ├── Transmission
│   ├── Battery
│   └── Braking
│
├── Driver
│   ├── Steering
│   ├── Braking
│   ├── Assistance
│   └── Controls
│
└── Economic
    ├── Price
    ├── Insurance
    ├── Maintenance
    └── Warranty
```

Then introduce concepts such as:

```text
hybrid battery
fuel efficiency
driver assistance system
insurance cost
battery warranty
regenerative braking
maintenance cost
```

where appropriate concepts legitimately belong to multiple fields.

The dataset MUST have explicit hidden ground truth.

Do not leak category names into the textual descriptions.

Produce both:

### Bare representation

```text
"hybrid battery"
```

### Contextual representation

For example:

```text
"The hybrid battery stores electrical energy and affects both
vehicle operation and long-term ownership costs."
```

Do not literally include the labels:

```text
mechanical
driver
economic
```

inside descriptions.

---

# 6. BASELINES

Every experiment must compare against simple baselines.

At minimum:

### Baseline A — raw cosine

For each item:

```text
similarity(item, field centroid)
```

and assign the highest field.

### Baseline B — hard k-means

Use the appropriate k and report:

* purity;
* cluster size;
* balance;
* ARI if appropriate;
* NMI if appropriate.

### Baseline C — multi-membership cosine

This is extremely important.

Do NOT compare the proposed system only against hard clustering.

Give raw cosine the fairest possible multi-membership baseline:

```text
item → similarity to every field
```

Then calibrate a threshold for membership.

The proposed architecture must beat or otherwise outperform this baseline in some meaningful dimension to justify its additional machinery.

---

# 7. MULTI-MEMBERSHIP REPRESENTATION

Modify the experimental classifier so that it emits the COMPLETE membership distribution.

Do not immediately collapse to:

```text
argmax(similarity)
```

Instead produce something like:

```rust
Vec<CellMembership>
```

where each entry contains:

```rust
domain
mode
score
```

Preserve the raw similarity values.

Experiment with:

* raw cosine;
* normalized scores;
* softmax;
* percentile/relative scores.

Do not arbitrarily choose one and declare success.

---

# 8. MEMBERSHIP THRESHOLD CALIBRATION

This is a known gap from Iteration 9.

Iteration 8 only demonstrated recall on four hand-picked cross-cutting items.

That is insufficient.

Determine whether multi-membership produces false positives.

Use:

```text
pure items
+
known cross-cutting items
```

and measure:

```text
true positive rate
false positive rate
precision
recall
F1
```

for multi-membership detection.

Test multiple thresholds.

Do NOT choose a threshold because it makes the output look good.

If the embedding produces many false dual-memberships, document that honestly.

---

# 9. CERTIFICATION GATE

Iteration 9 tested two candidate signals.

### Validated so far

Cluster-size balance:

```text
balance = min(size_a, size_b) / max(size_a, size_b)
```

The known comparison produced:

```text
GOOD 8/7  → 0.875
BAD 13/2  → 0.154
```

Therefore this is currently the strongest candidate certification signal.

Test thresholds such as:

```text
0.3
0.4
0.5
0.6
0.7
```

Do not assume 0.5 is correct.

Measure:

```text
false certification
false rejection
recursion precision
recursion recall
```

### Do NOT currently use naive silhouette/cohesion as the certification gate.

Iteration 9 found it ranked the bad split above the good split.

You may investigate improved coherence metrics, but they must earn their way back through controlled experiments.

---

# 10. CERTIFIED RECURSION

Implement recursion as:

```text
discover candidate partition
        ↓
evaluate partition
        ↓
certification gate
        ↓
      ┌─┴─┐
      │   │
    pass fail
      │   │
      ▼   ▼
   recurse stop
```

The system must NOT blindly recurse to a fixed depth.

A node is recursively expanded only if its candidate subdivision is sufficiently certified.

---

# 11. MULTI-MEMBERSHIP CHANGES THE STRUCTURE

Once multi-membership exists, the output is no longer necessarily a tree.

It becomes a DAG.

Example:

```text
Mechanical ───────┐
                  │
             hybrid battery
                  │
Economic ─────────┘
```

Therefore implement:

### visited-reference cache

The same semantic node must not be recursively expanded indefinitely through multiple membership paths.

Track at least:

```text
node identity
reference/cell identity
depth
```

or an equivalent canonical key.

Test for:

* duplicate expansion;
* cycles;
* repeated nodes;
* exponential growth;
* convergence.

---

# 12. RECURSIVE DISCOVERY

For every certified parent field:

```text
field
  ↓
collect members
  ↓
discover substructure
  ↓
candidate child fields
  ↓
certify
  ↓
recurse only when justified
```

The system should be able to produce something like:

```text
Vehicle
├── Mechanical
│   ├── Propulsion
│   ├── Energy Storage
│   └── Braking
│
├── Driver
│   ├── Controls
│   └── Assistance
│
└── Economic
    ├── Acquisition
    ├── Ownership Cost
    └── Warranty

hybrid battery
├── Mechanical / Energy Storage
└── Economic / Ownership Cost
```

This is an example of the desired *type* of structure, not a predetermined expected answer.

The agent must evaluate whatever structure is actually discovered.

---

# 13. STOPPING TESTS

Explicitly test whether recursion knows when to stop.

Include:

### True leaf

A group deliberately containing no known deeper ontology.

### Weakly structured leaf

A group where incidental semantic clustering is possible but no ground-truth ontology exists.

### Strongly structured node

A group with known substructure.

The system should:

```text
strong structure → recurse
weak/no structure → preferably stop
```

Do not equate any statistically discoverable split with an ontologically meaningful split.

---

# 14. REFERENCE SELECTION

This was an explicit gap in the previous research.

Test at least:

```text
fixed reference
random reference
highest-confidence reference
lowest-confidence / uncertainty reference
representative/centroid reference
```

Determine whether reference selection actually affects discovery quality.

Do not assume that uncertainty-based selection is superior.

---

# 15. CONTEXT EXPERIMENT

Repeat the integrated experiment with:

```text
bare terms
```

and:

```text
contextual sentences
```

Because Iterations 5–6 established that context is conditional.

Measure whether context improves:

* membership detection;
* field assignment;
* recursion;
* certification;
* hierarchy recovery.

Also test whether context causes pairwise reference methods to degrade through generic lexical scaffolding.

Do not cherry-pick the CAR result.

---

# 16. OPTIONAL JEPA TRACK

Only after the above system has a clean baseline should you investigate genuine JEPA-style learning.

The previous supervised predictor must NOT be called a JEPA result.

If implementing a JEPA-inspired experiment:

* use self-supervised targets;
* avoid perspective labels;
* separate context from target;
* predict latent representations rather than labels;
* use held-out data;
* compare directly against raw cosine and contextual embeddings;
* check collapse;
* report training/validation behavior.

The question is:

> Can a genuine self-supervised predictive latent objective learn relational/perspectival structure that raw embeddings do not already expose?

Do not assume the answer is yes.

Do not spend the majority of the mission on JEPA before the simpler synthesis is tested.

---

# 17. METRICS

Do not use purity alone.

Every clustering result should report:

```text
cluster sizes
purity
balanced accuracy where appropriate
ARI
NMI
```

For multi-membership:

```text
precision
recall
F1
false-positive rate
dual-membership recall
```

For recursion:

```text
correct parent recovery
correct child recovery
depth-wise accuracy
certification precision
certification recall
false recursion rate
missed recursion rate
```

For graph quality:

```text
duplicate node rate
cycle count
edge precision
edge recall
DAG validity
growth factor per depth
```

Also record:

```text
runtime
memory
number of embeddings
number of similarity computations
```

The goal is not merely a pretty ontology.

---

# 18. ABLATION MATRIX

Run the integrated system with components switched on/off.

At minimum:

```text
A. raw cosine
B. contextual embedding
C. multi-membership
D. certification
E. recursion
F. visited-reference protection
```

Test combinations such as:

```text
cosine only
cosine + multi-membership
cosine + certification
cosine + recursion
cosine + multi-membership + recursion
cosine + multi-membership + certification
cosine + multi-membership + certification + recursion
```

The final system must demonstrate that each additional mechanism earns its complexity.

If a component does not help, remove it.

---

# 19. FAIRNESS RULE

The central lesson from the previous research is:

> Do not compare an elaborate method against a weakly evaluated baseline.

Raw cosine has repeatedly survived because it is extremely strong.

Therefore the new architecture must be tested against:

```text
raw cosine
+
properly calibrated multi-membership cosine
```

not merely against hard k-means.

If raw cosine plus thresholding performs equally well, say so.

That result would be important.

---

# 20. DO NOT REPEAT THESE MISTAKES

Do NOT:

* resurrect Δ-direction merely because recursion is being tested;
* call a supervised predictor JEPA;
* use labels inside contextual sentences;
* evaluate only on training data;
* rely on purity without cluster sizes;
* select thresholds after seeing test results;
* cherry-pick successful examples;
* silently discard negative experiments;
* assume recursion is useful merely because it produces deeper trees;
* treat every cluster as an ontology;
* assume context universally improves embeddings;
* introduce a large schema migration before demonstrating value;
* confuse semantic similarity with ontological validity.

---

Create a reproducible experiment executable from the repository.


containing:

```json
  "baseline": "...",
  "metrics": {},
  "clusters": [],
  "memberships": [],
  "certification": {},
  "recursion": {},
  "graph": {},
  "runtime": {}
}
```

Also produce a human-readable report.

Keep all research experiments additive.

Do not modify production behavior unless explicitly justified and separately tested.

# 22. GIT DISCIPLINE

Work on a research branch.

Do not destroy previous experiments.

Preserve negative results.

Use focused commits.

For example:

```text
experiment: integrated multi-membership dataset

experiment: ablations
experiment: contextual representation
```

Do not rewrite history merely to make the research narrative cleaner.

---

# 23. SUCCESS CRITERIA

The mission is successful if it produces a defensible answer to:

> Can Physis recover hierarchical + cross-cutting ontology structure by preserving full semantic membership, certifying candidate partitions, and recursively refining only trustworthy regions?

Possible conclusions:

### SUPPORTED

The integrated architecture significantly outperforms appropriate baselines and demonstrates reliable hierarchical + multi-membership recovery.

### PARTIALLY SUPPORTED

Individual components work, but the complete system has important limitations.

### INCONCLUSIVE

Signals exist but the datasets/evaluation are insufficient to establish the hypothesis.

### FALSIFIED

The integrated architecture provides no meaningful improvement over properly calibrated raw-cosine baselines or produces unstable/invalid ontology structures.

A negative result is completely acceptable.

---

# 24. FINAL CONCEPTUAL MODEL

The working hypothesis after Iterations 1–9 is NOT:

```text
"Physis needs a cleverer similarity geometry."
```

It is:

```text
                  EMBEDDING
                      │
                      ▼
             semantic information
                      │
                      ▼
          ┌─────────────────────┐
          │ full field-membership│
          │     distribution     │
          └─────────────────────┘
                      │
                      ▼
             certification
                      │
             ┌────────┴────────┐
             ▼                 ▼
        trustworthy         unreliable
             │                 │
             ▼                 ▼
          recurse             stop
             │
             ▼
        substructure
             │
             ▼
      multi-membership DAG
```

The deeper architectural interpretation is:

> The embedding may already contain semantic structure. Physis' value may be in preserving, evaluating, certifying, connecting, and recursively elaborating that structure rather than replacing cosine with an arbitrary new metric.

Test this proposition rigorously.

Do not believe it merely because it sounds elegant.

Make the experiments decide.

This is the version I’d give the agent now: it treats the previous nine iterations as **research history**, makes the next experiment genuinely end-to-end, and gives the agent permission to kill the synthesis if calibrated cosine already does the job.

Here's the Iteration 12 result, running your full reframed mission end-to-end:

**Two real bugs caught before trusting anything** (consistent with this track's discipline): a coarse/fine ground-truth mismatch that silently zeroed the recall metric, and a calibration fallback that picked the *worst*-FPR delta among ties instead of the best. Both fixed and verified via raw similarity dumps before proceeding.

**Finding 1 — new, not seen before**: multi-membership calibration cost rises sharply with category granularity. Dataset D's three broad coarse fields calibrated cleanly (FPR=0.0). This dataset's six *fine* sibling categories (e.g., Propulsion vs. Energy, both under Mechanical) sit close enough in embedding space that catching all 5 cross-cutting items costs 27-53% false positives on genuinely single-membership items — concretely, `cruise_control` and `purchase_price` get spuriously flagged. This matters specifically for recursion: each depth inherently produces more fine-grained, more mutually-similar siblings, so a calibration tuned at one level should be expected to degrade at the next.

**Finding 2 — the important one**: a *third*, independently-designed dataset reproduced Iterations 10-11's "balanced but wrong" failure unprompted — the economic branch passes the balance gate (0.500, exactly at threshold) with ARI=-0.250 (worse than random agreement with truth). Three for three now. This is much stronger evidence it's a structural property of balance-based certification than either earlier instance alone.

The DAG visited-cache requirement was also reconfirmed a third time, and context hurt rather than helped on this dataset (a third data point after Iterations 5-6), consistent with "context helps in proportion to how poorly-represented the bare vocabulary already is" — these are standard, well-represented terms.

**Net verdict, per your Section 19 fairness rule**: no clean "baseline wins" — the multi-membership-cosine baseline can't even ask the question the full pipeline answers (discovering fine cells that don't exist yet). But the synthesis isn't ready for production either: two concrete, now multiply-confirmed problems block it (the certification blind spot, and calibration-degrades-with-granularity). Both are written up as the load-bearing next-experiments in FINAL_REPORT.md.

Both next-experiments are done, with a clean split outcome:

**Next Experiment 7 (calibration) — CLOSED.** The flat global threshold was conflating two different-scale problems. Splitting into a same-branch sibling check (near-zero cost here) and a separately-calibrated cross-branch check cut false-positive rate by roughly 4x (26.7%→6.7% bare, 53.3%→13.3% contextual) while keeping full recall. This is a real fix, recommended for production if the synthesis architecture is ever built.

**Next Experiment 6 (certification) — still open, but now conclusively so.** I tested the natural ensemble (balance ratio AND adaptive kNN-consistency) via an exhaustive threshold sweep across all 6 known real good/bad cases collected across five iterations. No threshold combination achieves perfect separation — best possible is 5/6, and the failure is structural, not a tuning issue: the threshold needed to reject the two known "balanced but wrong" splits also rejects the deliberately-imbalanced-but-correct control, because that control's kNN score is *lower* than the wrong splits' scores. This rules out "combine the two signals we have" as a fix — the next attempt needs a detector aimed specifically at "a few borderline items in an otherwise-consistent cluster," not another whole-cluster aggregate.

**Iteration 14 — certification signal search, now exhausted.** Following your own follow-through logic, I built a dedicated "few borderline items" detector (misfit fraction, worst-item margin, margin std-dev, plus a structurally different sub-bisection-contamination check). # PHYSIS RESEARCH MISSION — MULTI-MEMBERSHIP ONTOLOGY + CERTIFIED RECURSIVE DISCOVERY

You are an autonomous research-and-implementation agent working directly inside the Physis repository.

Your job is NOT to confirm the user's original hypothesis.

Your job is to determine, experimentally and rigorously, whether the following architecture can produce useful nested ontological structure:

```text
GLOBAL EMBEDDING
      ↓
FULL PER-CELL MEMBERSHIP
      ↓
MULTI-MEMBERSHIP REPRESENTATION
      ↓
CERTIFICATION GATE
      ↓
CONDITIONAL RECURSION
      ↓
NESTED SUB-FIELDS
      ↓
DAG / MULTI-PERSPECTIVE ONTOLOGY
```

You have already conducted a substantial research track. Treat its results as established experimental history. Do not silently redo or reinterpret them.

---

# 1. ESTABLISHED RESEARCH HISTORY

The previous research investigated whether reference-conditioned mechanisms could discover different ontological structures from different "points of view."

The important cumulative result is:

> Raw cosine similarity has NOT been beaten by any mechanism tested so far under a fair controlled comparison.

Specifically:

### Δ-magnitude

Δ-magnitude was shown to be mathematically reducible to cosine ranking for normalized embeddings.

Therefore:

```text
embedding
→ reference
→ Δ magnitude
```

does NOT constitute a novel semantic discovery mechanism.

Do NOT resurrect this mechanism as a production approach.

### Δ-direction

Δ-direction clustering was tested and beaten by simpler baselines.

Do NOT assume that subtracting a reference embedding creates a new ontology.

### Supervised predictor / "JEPA" experiment

The previous predictor experiment was NOT a genuine self-supervised JEPA experiment.

It used perspective labels and therefore contained a supervision confound.

It performed well on toy data but lost badly to raw cosine on the real 730-entry ontology.

Therefore:

* do NOT claim JEPA was falsified;
* do NOT claim the previous experiment demonstrated JEPA works;
* genuine self-supervised JEPA remains an open research question;
* however, JEPA is NOT the immediate next engineering priority.

### Contextual embeddings

Iteration 5 found a dramatic effect on the CAR corpus:

```text
bare k-means coarse purity:       0.476
contextual k-means coarse purity: 0.810
```

However, Iteration 6 replicated the experiment on an ANIMAL hierarchy and found:

```text
bare coarse purity:       0.667
contextual coarse purity: 0.667
```

Therefore:

> Context is not universally beneficial.

The refined hypothesis is:

> Context should help in proportion to how poorly represented the bare vocabulary already is.

Do not generalize the CAR result into a universal claim.

### Recursion

Iteration 7 tested recursion.

A critical methodological discovery was that purity alone concealed pathological cluster imbalance.

The real top-level k=2 clustering produced:

```text
13 items / 2 items
```

instead of the actual approximately:

```text
8 mammals / 7 birds
```

The resulting recursive pipeline demonstrated garbage-in-garbage-out behavior.

However, when the correct parent partition was supplied as an ORACLE:

```text
Mammal
  → Domestic / Predator
  purity = 0.625 bare / 0.750 contextual

Bird
  → Flying / Flightless
  purity = 0.714
```

This is the clearest positive signal for recursion found so far.

Conclusion:

> Recursive discovery is potentially useful, but only when the parent grouping is sufficiently reliable.

### Cross-cutting / multi-membership concepts

Iteration 8 produced the strongest positive result.

The corpus contained four deliberately cross-cutting concepts:

```text
fuel efficiency       → mechanical + economic
hybrid battery        → mechanical + economic
driver assistance     → mechanical + driver
warranty coverage     → driver + economic
```

Hard k-means assigned each to exactly one cluster.

That is a structural limitation.

However, raw embedding similarities already contained dual-membership information.

For all 4/4 concepts:

```text
similarity to weaker true field
    >
similarity to unrelated field
```

with positive dual margins:

```text
fuel efficiency       +0.037
hybrid battery        +0.013
driver assistance     +0.028
warranty coverage     +0.018
```

Therefore:

> The embedding itself appears to contain multi-field semantic information that hard single-label assignment destroys.

This is the most important surviving result.

---

# 2. CURRENT ARCHITECTURAL HYPOTHESIS

The research should now stop asking:

> "Can we invent a better geometry than cosine?"

and instead ask:

> "Can Physis preserve and exploit semantic information that cosine already contains but the current ontology/classification architecture throws away?"

The proposed architecture is:

```text
                    INPUT
                      │
                      ▼
              GLOBAL EMBEDDING
                      │
                      ▼
              CellClassifier
                      │
                      ▼
          FULL MEMBERSHIP VECTOR
                      │
          ┌───────────┼───────────┐
          ▼           ▼           ▼
       CELL A       CELL B       CELL C
        0.81         0.83         0.77
          │           │
          └──────┬────┘
                 ▼
         MULTI-MEMBERSHIP STATE
                 │
                 ▼
          CERTIFICATION GATE
                 │
        ┌────────┴────────┐
        ▼                 ▼
    CERTIFIED           REJECT
        │                 │
        ▼                 ▼
    RECURSE              STOP
        │
        ▼
   SUB-FIELD DISCOVERY
        │
        ▼
   REPEAT CONDITIONALLY
```

The crucial conceptual change is:

> A perspective is not necessarily a transformed embedding coordinate system.

Instead, a perspective can be represented as:

```text
object
  → relevance/membership across semantic fields
```

For example:

```text
fuel efficiency

mechanical = 0.804
economic   = 0.826
environmental = ...
```

rather than:

```text
fuel efficiency → economic
```

The output should preserve the entire semantic state.

---

# 3. PHYSIS-SPECIFIC ARCHITECTURAL FACTS

Inspect the actual repository before changing anything.

Previous research established:

### OntologyEntry

`src/models.rs` currently has:

```rust
domain: String
mode: String
```

This encodes single membership.

### CellClassifier

`classify.rs` already computes similarities against candidate cells before collapsing the result to one winning cell.

Investigate exactly where this information is discarded.

### CoherenceNode

`CoherenceNode.properties` is:

```text
serde_json::Map<String, Value>
```

and was restored as an extension point during PH-014.

Therefore a low-risk experimental implementation may store:

```text
properties["cell_memberships"]
```

without immediately changing the native schema.

For example:

```json
{
  "cell_memberships": [
    {
      "domain": "mechanical",
      "mode": "system",
      "score": 0.804
    },
    {
      "domain": "economic",
      "mode": "efficiency",
      "score": 0.826
    }
  ]
}
```

Do not make a large production schema migration unless experiments justify it.

# 6. BASELINES

Every experiment must compare against simple baselines.

```text
item → similarity to every field
```

Then calibrate a threshold for membership.

# 7. MULTI-MEMBERSHIP REPRESENTATION

Modify the experimental classifier so that it emits the COMPLETE membership distribution.

Do not immediately collapse to:

```rust
Vec<CellMembership>
```

Do not arbitrarily choose one and declare success.

# 8. MEMBERSHIP THRESHOLD CALIBRATION

This is a known gap from Iteration 9.

Iteration 8 only demonstrated recall on four hand-picked cross-cutting items.

That is insufficient.

Determine whether multi-membership produces false positives.


for multi-membership detection.

Test multiple thresholds.

If the embedding produces many false dual-memberships, document that honestly.

---

# 9. CERTIFICATION GATE

Iteration 9 tested two candidate signals.

### Validated so far

Cluster-size balance:

```text
balance = min(size_a, size_b) / max(size_a, size_b)
```

The known comparison produced:

```text
GOOD 8/7  → 0.875
BAD 13/2  → 0.154
```

Therefore this is currently the strongest candidate certification signal.

Test thresholds such as:

```text
0.3
0.4
0.5
0.6
0.7
```

Do not assume 0.5 is correct.

Measure:

```text
false certification
false rejection
recursion precision
recursion recall
```

### Do NOT currently use naive silhouette/cohesion as the certification gate.

Iteration 9 found it ranked the bad split above the good split.

You may investigate improved coherence metrics, but they must earn their way back through controlled experiments.

---

# 10. CERTIFIED RECURSION

Implement recursion as:

```text
discover candidate partition
        ↓
evaluate partition
        ↓
certification gate
        ↓
      ┌─┴─┐
      │   │
    pass fail
      │   │
      ▼   ▼
   recurse stop
```

The system must NOT blindly recurse to a fixed depth.

A node is recursively expanded only if its candidate subdivision is sufficiently certified.

---

# 11. MULTI-MEMBERSHIP CHANGES THE STRUCTURE

Once multi-membership exists, the output is no longer necessarily a tree.

It becomes a DAG.

Example:

```text
Mechanical ───────┐
                  │
             hybrid battery
                  │
Economic ─────────┘
```

Therefore implement:

### visited-reference cache

The same semantic node must not be recursively expanded indefinitely through multiple membership paths.

Track at least:

```text
node identity
reference/cell identity
depth
```

or an equivalent canonical key.

Test for:

* duplicate expansion;
* cycles;
* repeated nodes;
* exponential growth;
* convergence.

---

# 12. RECURSIVE DISCOVERY

For every certified parent field:

```text
field
  ↓
collect members
  ↓
discover substructure
  ↓
candidate child fields
  ↓
certify
  ↓
recurse only when justified
```

The system should be able to produce something like:

```text
Vehicle
├── Mechanical
│   ├── Propulsion
│   ├── Energy Storage
│   └── Braking
│
├── Driver
│   ├── Controls
│   └── Assistance

└── Economic
    ├── Acquisition
    ├── Ownership Cost
    └── Warranty

hybrid battery
├── Mechanical / Energy Storage
└── Economic / Ownership Cost
```

This is an example of the desired *type* of structure, not a predetermined expected answer.

The agent must evaluate whatever structure is actually discovered.

---

# 13. STOPPING TESTS

Explicitly test whether recursion knows when to stop.

Include:

### True leaf

A group deliberately containing no known deeper ontology.

### Weakly structured leaf

A group where incidental semantic clustering is possible but no ground-truth ontology exists.

### Strongly structured node

A group with known substructure.

The system should:

```text
strong structure → recurse
weak/no structure → preferably stop
```

Do not equate any statistically discoverable split with an ontologically meaningful split.

This was an explicit gap in the previous research.

Test at least:

```text
fixed reference
random reference
highest-confidence reference
lowest-confidence / uncertainty reference
representative/centroid reference
```

Determine whether reference selection actually affects discovery quality.

Do not assume that uncertainty-based selection is superior.

Because Iterations 5–6 established that context is conditional.

Measure whether context improves:

# 16. OPTIONAL JEPA TRACK

Only after the above system has a clean baseline should you investigate genuine JEPA-style learning.

The previous supervised predictor must NOT be called a JEPA result.

If implementing a JEPA-inspired experiment:

* use self-supervised targets;
* avoid perspective labels;
* separate context from target;
* predict latent representations rather than labels;
* use held-out data;
* compare directly against raw cosine and contextual embeddings;
* check collapse;
* report training/validation behavior.

The question is:

> Can a genuine self-supervised predictive latent objective learn relational/perspectival structure that raw embeddings do not already expose?

Do not assume the answer is yes.

Do not spend the majority of the mission on JEPA before the simpler synthesis is tested.

The goal is not merely a pretty ontology.

Run the integrated system with components switched on/off.


If a component does not help, remove it.

---

# 19. FAIRNESS RULE

The central lesson from the previous research is:

> Do not compare an elaborate method against a weakly evaluated baseline.

Raw cosine has repeatedly survived because it is extremely strong.

Therefore the new architecture must be tested against:

```text
raw cosine
+
properly calibrated multi-membership cosine
```

not merely against hard k-means.

If raw cosine plus thresholding performs equally well, say so.

That result would be important.

---

# 20. DO NOT REPEAT THESE MISTAKES

Do NOT:

* resurrect Δ-direction merely because recursion is being tested;
* call a supervised predictor JEPA;
* use labels inside contextual sentences;
* evaluate only on training data;
* rely on purity without cluster sizes;
* select thresholds after seeing test results;
* cherry-pick successful examples;
* silently discard negative experiments;
* assume recursion is useful merely because it produces deeper trees;
* treat every cluster as an ontology;
* assume context universally improves embeddings;
* introduce a large schema migration before demonstrating value;
* confuse semantic similarity with ontological validity.

Create a reproducible experiment executable from the repository.

containing:

# 22. GIT DISCIPLINE

Work on a research branch.

Do not destroy previous experiments.

Preserve negative results.

Use focused commits.

For example:

```text
experiment: integrated multi-membership dataset
experiment: membership calibration
experiment: certification gate
experiment: recursive DAG discovery
experiment: ablations
experiment: contextual representation
```

Do not rewrite history merely to make the research narrative cleaner.

---

# 23. SUCCESS CRITERIA

The mission is successful if it produces a defensible answer to:

> Can Physis recover hierarchical + cross-cutting ontology structure by preserving full semantic membership, certifying candidate partitions, and recursively refining only trustworthy regions?

Possible conclusions:

### SUPPORTED

The integrated architecture significantly outperforms appropriate baselines and demonstrates reliable hierarchical + multi-membership recovery.

### PARTIALLY SUPPORTED

Individual components work, but the complete system has important limitations.

### INCONCLUSIVE

Signals exist but the datasets/evaluation are insufficient to establish the hypothesis.

### FALSIFIED

The integrated architecture provides no meaningful improvement over properly calibrated raw-cosine baselines or produces unstable/invalid ontology structures.

A negative result is completely acceptable.

---

# 24. FINAL CONCEPTUAL MODEL

The working hypothesis after Iterations 1–9 is NOT:

```text
"Physis needs a cleverer similarity geometry."
```

It is:

```text
                  EMBEDDING
                      │
                      ▼
             semantic information
                      │
                      ▼
          ┌─────────────────────┐
          │ full field-membership│
          │     distribution     │
          └─────────────────────┘
                      │
                      ▼
             certification
                      │
             ┌────────┴────────┐
             ▼                 ▼
        trustworthy         unreliable
             │                 │
             ▼                 ▼
          recurse             stop
             │
             ▼
        substructure
             │
             ▼
      multi-membership DAG
```

The deeper architectural interpretation is:

> The embedding may already contain semantic structure. Physis' value may be in preserving, evaluating, certifying, connecting, and recursively elaborating that structure rather than replacing cosine with an arbitrary new metric.

Test this proposition rigorously.

Do not believe it merely because it sounds elegant.

Make the experiments decide.
