## Why I believe this one

Four positives died today under stronger controls. This one behaves differently in three ways:

1. **It strengthens under tighter control** — +6.31 across all strata, +6.64 restricted to the 35/40 where residual cosine imbalance is under 0.01. Artifacts shrink when you tighten; real effects don't.
2. **Independence is enforced, not assumed.** Every gloss token appearing in *either* entry's embedded text is discarded, so what's scored can only be information neither embedding received.
3. **Tightening from per-entry to strict pairwise exclusion changed nothing** — numbers byte-identical.

## One direction worth noticing

The signal is much stronger at **cell** level (+6.64) than **domain** level (+2.62). That's backwards from everything else in this track, where coarse targets are always easier. Shared independent description picks out *fine-grained* relatedness — which is precisely what a nesting architecture needs, and not what you'd expect from a generic topic effect.

## What it doesn't establish

The effect is **modest**: 0.0442 vs 0.0387 against a 0.0414 base rate. Real, not large.

And a WordNet gloss is a **definition** — decontextualised. Your case is *episodic*: "the dog ate my homework" supplies fangs-white-paper in **this** situation, not in general. So the episodic form is supported by analogy, not tested. The right corpus for that is physis's operational events, and it's empty here (`nodes: 0`, `edges: 0`).

This also validates the **corroboration premise**, not the depth-1 nesting architecture end to end.

## Where that leaves the architecture

Your three components now have distinct standing:

- **Depth-1 cap** — sound, solves smearing by construction, no test needed
- **Shared second-grade corroboration** — **supported**, z = +6.64, conditional on the source being independent
- **JEPA predictor as the third man** — untested, blocked on data scale, and pointing at the same place

All three roads lead to the operational corpus. The nesting layer needs independent encounters; the JEPA predictor needs volume and real co-occurrence. Neither can come from a 730-entry hand-authored ontology.

**So the unblocking move isn't more ML — it's getting `operational.rs` populated.** Once real events land there, both halves become testable with harnesses that already exist.

Pushed as `038302c`, two runs byte-identical.

While that compiles — here's what the paper actually gives us, ranked by whether it's testable here.

## The one that matters most: §7.1–7.2, the Yes-Man Problem

> *"An LLM asked to generate a deck-building game may mention `deck_building` as a mechanism yet include no cards in the components — structurally incoherent output that sounds like a deck-building game without being one."*

**That is precisely the hole I left open in `coverage` this morning.** My own caveat was *"a candidate that swallows records into a wrong cell scores identically to one that captures a real gap"* — coverage asks whether a record classifies *confidently*, not *rightly*.

Cheung's answer is a **dependency constraint map**: mechanism → required component; flag when the mechanism is present and the component absent. No labels, no LLM judgment, no embedding statistic — a structural rule that either holds or doesn't.

That's the same *family* as the two things that survived today (`coverage`, and the independent-substructure result): checks that aren't similarity statistics over the space that produced the candidate. It's the most promising lead for "coverage is not correctness."

## §6.1 Two-phase retrieval — and it predicts our own finding

> *"(1) ontology filtering, which narrows candidates to games sharing target mechanisms; then (2) semantic ranking by embedding similarity."*

**Structure filters, cosine ranks** — the inverse of what `classify.rs` does today (pure cosine over all 70 cells, structure never used as a gate). And it lines up with Iteration 35's odd result from an hour ago: shared independent substructure was much stronger at **cell** level (+6.64) than domain level (+2.62). Fine-grained structural filtering first is exactly what that shape recommends.

This is the most *directly implementable* item — it's a reordering of an existing pipeline, testable against the classification harness already built.

## §5.1 Professional anxieties — the honest caveat

Each agent carries a *concern* ("Balance Critic: what breaks when optimized?") to stop yes-man output. Genuinely interesting, but it's an **LLM-orchestration** technique, and it reintroduces exactly what I declined this morning: sampling nondeterminism into a pipeline whose determinism cost two production bug-fixes to establish. I'd hold this until there's a reason to pay that price.

## One thing to flag plainly

The paper's evaluation rests on **BoardGameGeek — 100,000 games** as the exemplar corpus. Its methods assume accumulated exemplars at that scale. physis has 730 hand-authored entries. The constraint-checking idea transfers regardless (it's a rule, not a model), but the retrieval-augmented half needs volume physis doesn't have — the same wall the JEPA discussion hit.

I'll report the populated corpus when the build lands.

## 3. Gantt + PDCA — you're describing something already half-built

`physis-core/src/process.rs` opens with:

> *"Bridges the loop: PLAN → ACTION → OBSERVATION → DEVIATION → INTERVENTION → OUTCOME"*

and line 188 says *"outcome of a process cycle or **PDCA iteration**"*. `ProcessGoal`, `ProcessPlan`, `ProcessTask`, `TaskState`, `StateTransition`, `ProcessDeviation`, `ProcessIntervention` all exist. `gantt.rs` in pro does dependency scheduling.

Why the framing is stronger than it might look, given today:

- **The only checks that survived were operational.** PDCA *is* the proposer/disposer loop with time added — Plan proposes, Check disposes, Act revises. Iteration 33's `coverage` is a single frozen Check step; PDCA generalises it.
- **`ProcessDeviation` → `ProcessIntervention` is a certification signal grounded in outcome**, not geometry. That's the family that keeps working.
- **"Streams of becoming" solves the third-man regress better than symlinks.** A relation with a start, duration, dependencies and a state is *addressable and finite by construction* — it's a scheduled item, not a Form needing another Form.
- It generalises Iteration 26b's lesson: don't re-derive, persist identity. An element with a *history* can't silently drift.

Same gap as everything else, though: `process_cycles: 0` — the model is unpopulated. The corpus I just built is the first real input it could take, and git history is genuinely PDCA-shaped (intent → diff → CI → fix/revert), which is one of the few outcome signals actually available.

Build running. While it does — **paper 1 & 2, read in full.**

## Paper 2 is the one that matters, and it formalizes your own proposal

You've been describing *nested facet systems* without the name. Cheung's second paper defines one precisely — and the definition contains exactly the two conditions that stop the third-man regress:

> **Definition 1.** A nested facet system is a finite set P of facets and a collection of refinements, such that
> 1. **Each element is the head of at most one refinement**
> 2. **The head of a refinement is not part of the body of the same refinement**

Condition 1 is your depth cap, generalised — it makes the structure finite without picking an arbitrary depth. Condition 2 blocks self-containment. Facets with no sub-facets are **primitive**; ones with sub-facets are **nested**. That is your `dog(fangs-white-paper)` with the regress formally closed.

## And it names the exact gap I left in `coverage`

> *"**Soundness** means all items below each facet are relevant to the facet. **Completeness** means any items or facets relevant to a facet are already contained in and accessible through it. Incomplete facets reduce **recall**; unsound facets reduce **precision**."*

This morning I shipped `coverage` with the caveat *"coverage is not correctness."* Cheung's vocabulary says why: **`coverage` is a completeness check. There is no soundness check.** A candidate that swallows records into the wrong cell is *unsound* — complete but imprecise.

And the paper points at the remedy:

> *"**non-lattice auditing methods can precisely identify and potentially fix such issues**"*

Non-lattice auditing is a purely **structural, label-free** defect detector: where a concept pair's minimal common ancestors aren't unique, the hierarchy fails to be a lattice, and those fragments are enriched for real modelling errors. No embeddings, no labels, no LLM. That's the same family as the two things that survived today.

**The honest caveat**: it needs a subsumption hierarchy. physis's grid is 5×14 flat with facets, not a taxonomy — so classic non-lattice auditing doesn't drop straight in. WordNet's hypernyms (Iteration 22) do provide one, and the `linkage` graph could be checked for lattice violations. That's a real lead, not a ready tool.

## Paper 1 and the repos

Paper 1 (Generative Ontology) is the ontology-as-grammar-for-LLM one — constraint checking (§7.2) is its transferable idea, retrieval-augmented generation needs BoardGameGeek-scale exemplars we don't have.

The three repos at the end are Benny Cheung's own: **FoodInsight**, **PyDeepDream**, **PyDeepStyle**. The latter two are image-ML (DeepDream/style transfer) — not relevant. FoodInsight may be ontology-applied; I haven't fetched any of them.

**pydantic 2.13.5 / pydantic-core 2.46.5 are installed**; `dspy` is not, so paper 1's pipeline isn't runnable as written.

I'll report the install once the build lands.

## What I built

| scorer | AUC (0.5 = chance) |
|---|---|
| NON-LATTICE (structural) | 0.460 |
| ORPHAN-RATE | 0.433 |
| ANCESTRY (structural) | 0.570 |
| **COSINE (baseline)** | **0.741** |

Within cosine strata: 0.473 and 0.529. Structure adds nothing.

## Two things that make this a real attempt rather than a lazy null

**I found a bug in my own criterion first.** The initial version scored `|MCA| != 1`, which lumps *"no common ancestor at all"* (unrelated) together with *"more than one minimal common ancestor"* (the actual lattice violation). Opposite situations. With it stated correctly, the diagnostics show the method **is** applicable and firing — 13.7% orphan pairs, 64.0% clean lattices, **22.2% genuinely non-lattice.** It isn't inert. It just doesn't track misfiling.

**Then I diagnosed the failure with a size-matched control** instead of guessing:

| | real cells | shuffled grouping |
|---|---|---|
| pairs with \|MCA\| = 1 | 0.653 | 0.668 |
| mean ancestor Jaccard | 0.133 | 0.117 |

**physis's 5×14 grid is not taxonomically structured.** Its cells are indistinguishable from an arbitrary regrouping under taxonomic measures.

## Why that's the actual finding

Non-lattice auditing audits **taxonomies** — it finds missing or redundant IS-A links in a deep subsumption hierarchy. physis's grid groups by **function** (HEAL/WORK, BOND/REST), not by kind. A kind-hierarchy has nothing to say about a function-grid, and no tuning of this audit would change that. Cheung's method is sound; the substrate is wrong.

**So the soundness check cannot come from a taxonomic referent — there's no taxonomy here to audit.** If cells group by function, soundness needs a *functional* referent: does this entry behave like its cellmates *in use*? That's operational — and operational is where both surviving checks in this entire track already live (`coverage`, and Iteration 33's disposer).

That's a narrower and more useful place to look than another structural statistic. It also means the operational corpus I populated earlier isn't a side quest — it's the only substrate a soundness check could stand on.

Pushed as `fefef45`, two runs byte-identical.

## `a0d28a4` — the paper's method, applied faithfully

> *"the ontology is not only a classification vocabulary but a validation contract — an output the grid cannot name is structure-shaped noise"*

That's Cheung's §7.1/§7.2 (the Yes-Man Problem and Ontological Constraint Checking) landed on a real surface. The design choices are good ones:

- **Rejections are counted, and the raw pool keeps every rejected dream** — *"the subconscious keeps what the contract refuses to propose."* Epistemically right: the gate filters what gets proposed without destroying what it refused. Same principle as preserving negative results.
- **`weakest_accepted`** exposes how much contract slack a batch used — that's the number that tells you whether the threshold is doing work or rubber-stamping.
- **Backward compatible throughout**: serde-defaulted verdict, legacy dreams load unchanged, `min_cosine` absent = exact prior behaviour.

**It also fixes a real numerical bug** I'd flag on its own merits: the schedule-dream generator scaled the goal vector by `1.0 + boost * 0.1` without renormalizing, violating `VectorEmbed`'s L2-normalized invariant. Cosine against an unnormalized vector is distorted — every downstream comparison from that generator was subtly wrong.

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
CLAUDE  [2026-09-07 11:03:38]
------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Confirmed, and the square answers your question more precisely than I expected — because it has **exactly three relation types**, and they map one-to-one onto the three outcomes you want to tell apart.

## The mapping

| square relation | positions | your case |
|---|---|---|
| **contradiction** | S1 / ¬S1 (diagonal) | **two dogs** — mutually exclusive, identity blocked |
| **contrariety** | S1 / S2 (complex axis) | **variation** — opposed but co-possible, same dog, owned |
| **implication** | ¬S2 → S1 (deixis) | **becoming** — dog0 → dog1 along a path |

That's the discriminator you were reaching for. A contradiction *forbids* identity across occurrences; a contrariety *permits* it; an implication *traces* it. The ± sign alone isn't enough — it's the **relation type** that tells you which case you're in.

**physis only implements one of the three.** `dual()` gives the contrary (CREATE↔DESTROY, LEARN↔GUIDE, SENSE↔BRAINSTORM). There is no contradiction relation and no implication relation. That's a small, concrete gap — and it's exactly the two you'd need to distinguish "two dogs" from "becoming."

## Your mistake/variation distinction is the sharpest part

*"un-remarked" vs "owned and built upon"* is operationalizable in a way nothing else in this track has been: a deviation that occurs once and is never taken up is noise; one that **recurs and is developed** is a variation. The judge isn't a similarity statistic or a reference ontology — it's **the document's own subsequent behaviour**.

That's the same family as the only two checks that have survived here (operational/outcome-based), applied to text. And it's literally PDCA: physis-core already models `ProcessDeviation → ProcessIntervention → ProcessOutcome`. A deviation that gets acted on and persists is a change; one that doesn't is a mistake. Your "PDCA check inside the pentagram" is that loop, at the scale of a term's occurrences.

## What makes this testable — and it's unusually lucky

Nothing in this track has used **order**. Clustering says "two clusters" whether or not there was a transition; a *path* through the intermediate states is what separates becoming from two-dogs, and that's only visible in sequence.

The corpus I populated is time-ordered (2026-05-22 → 2026-09-07) — **and it contains this session.** So there are terms with datable, known meaning-changes:

- **"coverage"** — a generic word until ~08:00 today, when it became a module name
- **"linkage"** — same, on 09-06
- **"disposer"** — didn't exist in this sense before today

Those are real `dog0 → dog1` events with ground truth I can verify independently, plus control terms that shouldn't have shifted. That's a genuine test of whether order-aware analysis separates *becoming* from *two entities* — not another synthetic perturbation.

Want me to build that? The pieces are all here: the corpus has timestamps, Iteration 37 already numbers instances, and `dual()` gives one of the three relations to extend.

**Sources:** [Semiotic square (Wikipedia)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semiotic_square) · [Hébert, *The Semiotic Square*](https://www.revue-texto.net/Parutions/Livres-E/Hebert_AS/2.Semiotic-Square.pdf) · [Signo — Greimas: The Semiotic Square](https://www.signosemio.com/pages/greimas/semiotic-square.php)

CLAUDE  [2026-09-07 11:10:32]

Module is in core and passing. While the experiment builds, what got built:

**`physis_core::becoming`** — the first thing in this engine that reads **order**.

The core claim, and its test:

```
AAAAAABBBBBB   two senses, time-SEPARATED  -> Becoming
ABABBABAABAB   two senses, INTERLEAVED     -> Split
```

Identical partitions, identical counts. A clusterer cannot tell them apart — it reports "two senses" for both. Only the sequence separates them, and the statistic for *"are these labels clumped or alternating"* is the **Wald–Wolfowitz runs test**, which has a real null distribution. So the verdict ships a z-score rather than a threshold I picked.

Two design choices worth flagging:

- **No RNG anywhere.** 2-means seeds from the farthest-apart pair with index tie-breaks, so the same input always yields the same verdict. A verdict that changes between runs isn't a verdict — that lesson cost two production bugs earlier today.
- **Mistake vs variation is decided by uptake, not similarity.** A deviation that never recurs is a mistake; one that a later occurrence resembles is a variation the text built on. The judge is the document's own subsequent behaviour — no reference ontology, no labels. That's your "owned" criterion, and it's the same operational family as the only two checks that have survived this track.

Seven unit tests pass, including `verdicts_are_reproducible` and the pair that distinguishes the two orderings above.

**On the semiotic square**: `dual()` gives you contrariety. What `becoming` adds is effectively the *implication* relation — a traced path from dog0 to dog1 — and `Split` is where contradiction would sit. So the three square relations now have three homes, though only `dual()` and this are implemented; a genuine contradiction relation is still missing.

## Built + integrated

**`physis_core::becoming`** shipped (`be69b7b`), 7 unit tests, **148 lib tests pass**. The core claim is unit-proven: `AAAAAABBBBBB` → Becoming, `ABABBABAABAB` → Split. ## Tested — 1/8, and the two failures identify the cause

I ran it twice, and they failed in **opposite** directions, which is what pinpoints the problem:

| attempt | context | result |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | whole event text | everything Becoming/Split, nothing Stable, change points at index 0–1 |
| 2 | ±12-word window | BECOMING→Stable (sep 0.085–0.15); SPLIT→Stable but **runs z −3.37, −5.40**; STABLE→Split, **sep 0.806** for "test" |

Attempt 1 was my error — embedding a whole paragraph clusters *document genre*, not word sense.

Attempt 2 shows the real limit: **`separation` measures breadth of use, not sense multiplicity.** "test" has one meaning across wildly varied contexts and scores 0.806; "key" carries genuinely distinct senses and scores 0.087.

**So: `becoming` is correct *given* a sense partition; it cannot produce one.** The runs test does what it claims. Word sense induction is the unsolved input.

## The literature — and it's a named open problem

This is **SemEval-2020 Task 1: Unsupervised Lexical Semantic Change Detection** — 33 teams, 186 systems, gold standards in four languages. The organisers name **evaluation itself** as the field's most pressing problem, because gold standards didn't exist. Best reported: **66.5% accuracy / 51.8 Spearman**.

So 1/8 on eight self-chosen terms is a first attempt at an open problem, not a bug to tune.

Two actionable findings, and they **conflict** — worth knowing before picking:
- TemporalTeller: **SGNS + Temporal Referencing** best; DIACR-Ita finds **BERT does not outperform SGNS**
- UiO-UvA: contextualised *beats* static, ELMo over BERT

My contextual-window approach is squarely the *contested* family. **Temporal referencing is the untried alternative**, and it's cheap.

## Your two module finds — both real, and better than what I built

**`chronos`** (`src/machines/chronos.rs`) is your pentagram, already implemented: *"reduce to recurrence primitives (rhythms); predict the next instance; flag rhythms about to break."* `Rhythm` carries `period_days` (median inter-arrival) and `confidence = regularity (1 − coefficient of variation) × evidence`, with `slipping(rhythms, now, tolerance)` as the attention flag. That **is** "regular tempo = coherent, variatio = pay attention" — and it works on *labels*, which sidesteps exactly the sense-induction problem that sank `becoming`.

**Seven `noe-` repos**, all recovered archives from 2026-08-31: `noe-physis`, `noe-philosophy`, `noe-merlin`, `noe-agora`, `noe-aura`, `noe-llmwiki_physis`, `noe-PokeClaw`.

The obvious next move is combining chronos with becoming — chronos gives rhythm over labels (no induction needed), becoming gives trajectory over senses (blocked on induction). Feeding chronos's *labels* rather than induced clusters into the runs test would sidestep the failure entirely. Want me to try that, or look inside `noe-physis` first?

**Sources:** [SemEval-2020 Task 1 (arXiv 2007.11464)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2007.11464) · [TemporalTeller](https://aclanthology.org/2020.semeval-1.27/) · [UiO-UvA contextualised embeddings](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2005.00050) · [CL-IMS @ DIACR-Ita](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2011.07247) · [Survey on contextualised semantic shift detection](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2304.01666)

Your n-gram idea targets the exact failure I just diagnosed, and I want to name why before I move on: **discretization**. Cosine separation couldn't tell "one sense used broadly" from "two senses" because it's continuous. That's paradigmatic analysis — symbolic, not geometric, which matters after nine geometric failures.

While that runs, the state of play on your three threads:

**chronos integration — built.** The bridge is a new core function, `becoming::classify_labeled`, which takes ordered **discrete labels** instead of vectors. That's the direct fix for Iteration 39's diagnosed failure: cluster separation couldn't tell "one sense used broadly" from "two senses" because it's continuous, but a label is an exact symbol — same/different is *decided*, not measured. 12 unit tests pass, including `broad_use_of_one_sense_is_stable_under_labels`, the precise case that defeated the vector version.

Experiment 40 then reads one label sequence **two ways**:
- **chronos** as tempo — `detect_rhythms` (period = median inter-arrival, confidence = regularity × evidence) and `slipping` as the attention flag
- **becoming** as melody — are the two leading senses time-separated or interleaved

Labels come from `CellClassifier` on each local window, so the sense inventory is the hand-authored grid rather than an induced clustering. That's the one thing this track measured to be stable.

One thing I'll flag now rather than after: **70 cells is a blunt sense inventory.** It's a functional grid, not a lexicon — asking it to distinguish "licence key" from "HashMap key" is asking more than it was built for. If this comes back weak, that's the most likely reason, not the runs test.

**Your n-gram idea is the better version of this**, and I want to say why concretely. Cell labels are *coarse and fixed* — 70 buckets for all of English. An n-gram signature is *exact and unbounded*: "test the hypothesis" and "test suite passes" are different symbols, full stop. Broad use produces many non-recurring n-grams; two senses produce two *recurring families*. And the substitution test then gives you the relation type directly — co-varying in the same slot = synonym, same slot but never together = contrary, partial overlap = simile. That's paradigmatic analysis, and physis's `semiotic_ontology.json` already carries `Paradigmatic` and `Syntagmatic` as entries.

It's also symbolic rather than geometric, which matters: nine geometric mechanisms have failed in this track, and the two things that survived (`coverage`, and the independent-substructure result) were not similarity statistics.

I'll report Experiment 40's numbers when they land, then that's the natural next build.

**Committed** (`6d45946` in pro; core was already pushed at `ad83325`).

## Where the becoming/chronos line stands

**Three attempts at the sense partition, three distinct failures — all at the same step:**

| attempt | partition source | how it failed |
|---|---|---|
| 39a | whole event text | clusters document **genre**, not sense |
| 39b | ±12-word window vectors | separation measures **breadth of use** |
| 40 | ontology cell labels | labels **scatter** — 20 distinct over 88 occurrences |

**The runs test has never been the failing part.** Its unit tests separate `AAAAAABBBBBB` from `ABABBABAABAB` — a distinction no clusterer can make. Every failure has been in producing a partition to hand it.

## chronos itself came out fine

Worth separating from the verdict: periods of 5.1d (`key`), 6.7d (`coverage`), 6.9d (`core`), confidence 0.35–0.61 for the regular ones, and **0.12 for `test`** — which genuinely is irregular. Tempo detection over labels doesn't need sense induction, which is exactly why it works where becoming's input doesn't. Your instinct to point me at it was right; it just solves the half that was already solvable.

## What's shipped and what I'd do next

`physis_core::becoming` is in core with 12 tests — `classify` (vectors) and `classify_labeled` (discrete labels). The module is sound and honest about its precondition: **it is correct given a sense partition; it cannot produce one.**

**Your n-gram proposal is the one remaining principled option**, and after three failures I think it's the right call rather than a fourth variation:

- An n-gram is an **exact symbol** — broad use gives many non-recurring signatures, two senses give two *recurring families*. That's precisely the distinction all three attempts failed to make.
- Substitutability in the same slot gives the **relation type** directly: co-varying = synonym, same slot but never together = contrary, partial overlap = simile.
- It's **symbolic, not geometric** — and nine geometric mechanisms have failed here, while the survivors (`coverage`, independent substructure) were not similarity statistics.
- It needs **no embedder at all**, so none of the three failure modes above can recur.

One caveat I'd want to test early: n-gram sparsity. With 200k words, exact 3-grams may be too rare to form recurring families — which is why the "variation, synonyms" part of your idea matters, since it's what lets near-misses count as the same signature.

Say the word and I'll build it.

**Recurrence exists** — 11–83%, so signatures do form families. Not the wipeout I was worried about.

**But my "variation" allowance made it slightly worse, not better.** Skip-grams (nearest content word, ignoring function words) scored *below* exact adjacency on every single term. Discretization does what you said it would.

Viable, with one dependency: the synonym/contrary layer isn't a refinement, it's required — exact n-grams are too sparse alone (21% recurrence on `coverage`), and naive loosening backfires.

## What actually worked

I held everything to one bar: **survived a control designed to kill it.** Five positives died to stronger controls today, so that distinction is what makes the rest worth anything.

**Shipped and load-bearing** — deterministic ontology iteration (a real production bug, fixed *twice*; the first fix wasn't a total order); `linkage`; **`coverage`** (re-adding an existing entry scores **0 of 2000**, interpolations t = +12.11); **`becoming`'s runs test** (separates `AAAAAABBBBBB` from `ABABBABAABAB` — no clusterer can); **`chronos`** (periods 5.1–6.9d, confidence 0.12 for `test`, which genuinely is irregular).

**Findings that hold** — re-derivation destroys ~80% of structure on +25% data; nothing survives an embedder swap (ARI ~0.10); independent-source substructure corroborates links at z = +6.64 *and strengthened under tighter control*; interpolation beats random pairs but the specific pair choice doesn't; all six negatives are byte-reproducible.

n-gram discretization is filed as **promising, not validated** — including the measured trap that naive variation makes it worse.

## Why I think you're right about the combination

It's not optimism, it's that the failure modes interlock:

```
becoming   runs test WORKS, cannot produce a partition
n-grams    produce an EXACT partition — no induction, no embedder
chronos    works on LABELS — and signatures ARE labels
```

n-grams supply the vocabulary both others need. That's the whole join, and it's why this is worth building rather than a fourth variation on a statistic that's failed three times.

## The plan, with kill criteria

Six stages, each with a **number that stops the work** rather than an impression that continues it. Two are worth calling out:

**Stage 0 comes before any building: get an external gold standard.** This track currently rests on eight terms I chose myself, on a corpus of one project's own documentation with no real polysemy. SemEval-2020's organisers name evaluation as the field's bottleneck. Without ≥30 terms nobody here authored, every later result is unfalsifiable — which is precisely how Iterations 39 and 40 went.

**Stage 5 has a genuinely held-out test.** Relation typing must recover `dual()`'s existing oppositions — CREATE↔DESTROY, LEARN↔GUIDE, SENSE↔BRAINSTORM — **without being given them**. Those pairs are hand-authored and independent of any corpus statistic, so recovering them is real evidence rather than a fit.

And three standing constraints on everything: pin the embedder, persist rather than re-derive, and use **construction-matched** controls — three positives died to that last distinction alone today.

The honest read on sequencing: Stage 0 is unglamorous and it's the one that decides whether any of the rest means anything.

CLAUDE  [2026-09-07 12:08:21]

Added as item 34, pushed (`b449e22`).

## The argument for trying it

Not "it's next in the paper" — there's a specific reason it should work where Iteration 38 didn't:

**The thing that made non-lattice auditing inapplicable is the thing that makes this applicable.** Non-lattice auditing needs a taxonomy, and I measured that physis's grid isn't one (real cells indistinguishable from a size-matched shuffle). But Cheung's §7.2 mechanism is a **dependency map** — *"deck building requires cards"* — and that constraint is **functional, not taxonomic**. It says "if X is present then Y must be", which is exactly the shape of a grid that groups by function rather than kind.

## The real design question isn't the library

physis is Rust; typed validation needs no Python at all. **Pydantic's actual contribution is that schemas are declarative and round-trippable through JSON Schema** — which would let constraints live in `config/`, authored and versioned alongside the ontology they constrain, rather than compiled in.

Given that hand-authored cells are the one thing this whole track measured to be stable, **constraints-as-authored-data is the option the evidence supports.** That's the decision to make, and it's independent of whether Python ever enters the build.

## Prior art in-tree, and its limit

The other agent's `DreamContract` already implements the validation-contract idea — but it gates on a **cosine threshold**. That's a *nameability* gate, not a *dependency* gate. Iteration 38 independently vindicated that cosine choice (AUC 0.741 beat every structural scorer I built), so the contract is right; the constraint layer is what's missing.

## The gate reuses evidence already in hand

Iteration 38's harness exists: **91 entries deterministically misfiled, exact ground truth, and the geometric baseline already measured on the identical split.** So the bar isn't "find something" — it's **beat AUC 0.741**, the detector already shipped. Below that it adds nothing over `coverage` + cosine and shouldn't ship.

Stated up front in the item: a dependency constraint catches *structural* incoherence — a mechanism without its component. It does **not** catch a semantically wrong but structurally complete entry. That harder half of soundness stays open after nine geometric mechanisms, non-lattice auditing, and this.

Also recorded: `dspy` isn't installed, so the paper's generation pipeline isn't runnable as written and is out of scope regardless.

The plan now has 34 items with items 31–34 as the live front, each with a kill criterion. Item 34's gate is the cheapest to reach because the harness already exists — worth knowing if you want a quick decisive result before committing to the n-gram build.

## Result: NOT SUPPORTED, at the gate the plan specified

```
CONSTRAINT (axis_name/category unattested in domain)   AUC = 0.433
COSINE     (geometric baseline)                        AUC = 0.741
```

Doesn't beat chance, let alone the 0.741 bar. Same injection, same split, same baseline as Iteration 38 — so it's directly comparable.

## The diagnosis is the useful part

**A constraint is a detector only if it stays quiet on correct data.** Under the *true* assignment this one fires on **548/957 correctly-filed entries (57%)**. A rule that flags most correct cases can't flag incorrect ones.

Root cause: `axis_name` has 553 distinct values over 957 entries, `category` 155. **They're free text, not controlled vocabularies** — and Cheung's mechanism needs enums on *both* sides of the dependency ("deck building" → "cards").

The one genuinely enum-like field fails the *opposite* way: `axis_kind`, 41 values, fires on only **4%** of correct entries — well-formed — but purity toward domain is 0.331 against 0.304 shuffled. **Controlled but uninformative.**

**physis has no field that is both controlled and informative about the grid**, which is precisely what a dependency constraint requires.

## Why that's actionable rather than just another null

This one names its own prerequisite. The blocker isn't the method — the reasoning (functional constraints suit a functional grid where taxonomic ones don't) still holds, which is why I kept it in the report as 34b. The blocker is **the data model**: there's no enum that correlates with domain or mode.

So this route re-opens by **authoring that vocabulary** — an `axis_kind`-style controlled field that actually carries grid information — not by trying a fourth checker. That's ontology work, and hand-authored structure is the one thing this track has consistently found reliable.

One methodological note worth keeping: the cardinality-matched shuffles were load-bearing again. Raw purity said `unit` predicts mode at 0.761 — entirely because 572 distinct values over 957 entries is a near-unique key. Without that control I'd have reported a strong constraint that was pure artifact.

If you want dspy for real, it's `pip install dspy-ai` plus a model on the :8089 gateway — but the paper's generation pipeline tests a different question than the one that was blocking us.

give me a command to install dspy on debian

CLAUDE  [2026-09-07 12:16:52]
------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Debian 13 marks the system Python `EXTERNALLY-MANAGED`, so `pip3 install dspy` will refuse. You have `uv`, which is the fastest route:

```bash
uv venv ~/.venvs/dspy && uv pip install -p ~/.venvs/dspy/bin/python dspy
```

Then run with `~/.venvs/dspy/bin/python`, or `source ~/.venvs/dspy/bin/activate`.

Stdlib equivalent if you'd rather not use uv:

```bash
python3 -m venv ~/.venvs/dspy && ~/.venvs/dspy/bin/pip install dspy
```

Two notes:

- The PyPI package is now **`dspy`**; `dspy-ai` is the legacy name and still resolves, so either works but `dspy` is current.
- **dspy alone won't run anything** — it needs a configured LM, and your gateway at `:8089` currently returns `{"data": []}`. You'd need a model served there (or an API key) before `dspy.configure(lm=...)` does anything. Worth knowing before you spend the install on it.

I'd also flag: the gate that mattered didn't need dspy, and it already returned a decisive answer (constraints at AUC 0.433 vs the 0.741 bar, blocked on physis having no field that's both a controlled vocabulary and grid-informative). dspy would let us test the *paper's generation pipeline* — a different question — so it's worth installing if you want that, but it won't reopen item 34.

## The tweak you asked for — and it was the right idea

Item 34 said the blocker was that physis has no field both **controlled** and **informative about domain×mode**. There is one, and I hadn't used it: `config/mode_anchors_ontology.json` — **70 entries, exactly one per cell**, with hand-authored hints defining what each cell means, written separately from the 957 corpus entries. Excluded from the tested set so no anchor matches itself.

**It still doesn't work: 0.532, barely above chance, well under cosine.**

The likely reason is worth more than the number. The anchors define each cell **in their own idiom** — `HEAL/LIFT` is "deadlift, squat heavy, push to the limit" — while corpus entries are written in theirs. Lexical overlap between two independently-authored vocabularies is thin. **That's the same sparsity that defeated exact n-grams, arriving from a different direction.**

So it refines item 34's prerequisite rather than satisfying it: what's needed isn't merely a controlled vocabulary, but one drawn from **the same surface forms the entries actually use**.

## On dspy

Pull any model (`ollama pull qwen3:8b` or similar) and it becomes usable — at which point the interesting experiment is generating a cell vocabulary *in the entries' own idiom*, which is exactly the prerequisite this run just identified.

That's the honest state: the tweak was well-aimed, it failed for a specific and now-understood reason, and the thing that would fix it is the one thing dspy would actually be good for.

USER  [2026-09-07 12:39:11]

qwen3:4b pulled

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
CLAUDE  [2026-09-07 12:42:08]
------------------------------------------------------------------------------

While that warms up, here's the design — and the key point is that **dspy has to earn its place against a no-LLM control**, same as everything else today.

**The prerequisite the last run identified:** a cell vocabulary in *the entries' own idiom*, not the anchors' idiom.

**The circularity trap I have to avoid:** deriving a cell's vocabulary from its entries and then testing whether those entries match it is meaningless. So — deterministic train/test split within each cell, vocabulary built from the training half only, scored on the held-out half plus the injected misfilings.

**Three arms, differing in exactly one thing:**

| arm | vocabulary source |
|---|---|
| **A** (no LLM control) | top IDF-weighted terms from the cell's *training* entries |
| **B** (dspy/qwen3:4b) | LLM given the cell's anchor + training examples, asked for *additional* terms in the same register |
| baseline | cosine to cell centroid — **AUC 0.650** on this exact split |

The question isn't "does the vocabulary work" — it's **does B beat A?** If simple term-frequency from a training half does the job, the LLM contributes nothing. If A fails and B succeeds, the LLM's contribution is precisely generalizing *beyond observed surface forms*, which is a real and testable claim.

**On determinism** — I flagged this morning that LLM sampling would undo the two production bugs I fixed to get byte-identical runs. The architecture that avoids it: `temperature=0`, generate the vocabulary **once**, write it to disk as an artifact, and have the scoring read that file. The LLM becomes an authoring assistant whose output is committed and reviewable, not a runtime dependency. Everything downstream stays deterministic and reproducible.

That's the same shape as my earlier recommendation about LLM-as-authoring-tool, so it's consistent rather than a special case.

dspy→ollama works, but two real problems: **139.7s for a trivial prompt**, and the reply was *empty* — qwen3 is a reasoning model that spent all 120 tokens on `reasoning_content`. At that rate, 70 cells ≈ 2.7 hours. Testing the fix:

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
CLAUDE  [2026-09-07 12:46:15]
------------------------------------------------------------------------------

The generator script is written and ready. Its design, so you can judge it before it runs:

**The circularity guard.** Every cell splits deterministically into train/test halves (even/odd by sorted name). Vocabulary comes from **train only**; scoring happens on the held-out half plus the injected misfilings. Building a cell's vocabulary from its entries and then testing those same entries would prove nothing.

**The control that makes dspy falsifiable.** Two vocabularies, identical inputs, one difference:

- **A — frequency**: top IDF-weighted terms from the cell's train entries. No LLM.
- **B — generated**: qwen3:4b given the anchor + train examples, asked for *additional* terms in the same register.

The question isn't whether a vocabulary helps — it's **does B beat A**. If plain term frequency suffices, the model contributes nothing and shouldn't be in the pipeline. And both get measured against the standing cosine baseline of **0.650** on the same split.

**Pydantic does real work here**, which is the paper's actual mechanism: `CellVocab` constrains the output to a list of lowercase single words, so a model that returns prose or phrases fails the contract rather than silently poisoning the artifact. That's "ontology as validation contract" pointed at the generator itself.

**Determinism.** `temperature=0`, generated **once**, written to a JSON artifact the scorer reads. The LLM is an authoring assistant whose product is committed and reviewable — not a runtime dependency. Everything downstream stays byte-reproducible, which is the property two production bug-fixes bought this session.

I can run arm A (`--no-llm`) immediately regardless of how the latency test resolves — and honestly, if A alone clears 0.650, that's the more useful outcome anyway.
